                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.40                           | The route's figures run **across the page as one strip**, and the flight table is its own panel.                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Measured: the detail is 1141px wide and 168px tall, where a stacked column of four numbers and two footnotes was mostly empty space with everything below it pushed down. Laid out sideways — who, then the numbers, then the footnotes on one line each — it says the same things in a third of the height, and the chart below gets that height back. The table left the chart's panel at the same time: the chart answers "is this route cheaper than usual", the table answers "cheaper on what, and which ones moved". Two questions, two boxes — and the table is about to grow sorting and filters, which a heading inside someone else's panel could not carry.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.41                           | The glass globe shows **places** through itself, not lines — refining 12.27's split.                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | The far half of an arc is now cut at the limb rather than drawn dimmed. A faint curve across the back reads as a curve across the front, and on the way it crosses everything genuinely in front of it. A dot does not: knowing an endpoint is round there is worth something, so the airports stay, at 0.3 opacity. `splitByHorizon` still does the cutting — that is what makes a line stop cleanly at the horizon instead of at whichever sample happened to fall near it. Measured with LIM–NRT and LIM–SYD in view: 5 arc runs drawn, none marked behind, and NRT and SYD both on screen dimmed.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.42                           | The route panel's footnotes became **a second box of figures**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | What was two sentences — the board, then the collector's own record — is now labelled figures read at the same glance as the prices rather than after them. The heartbeat count in particular deserved it: a stretch of archive with no new points means either no price movement or no collector, and only that number tells them apart. It costs height (237px against 168), which is the honest price of turning prose into a grid; the column minimum is set from measured text, and the unit matters — this app sets `--font-size-base: 125%`, so a rem is 20px, and a minimum of 9rem was one pixel-count too wide for four money figures to share a row. | 12.43                           | The route header carries **which route, when, where and last look**; every figure sits on one row.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | The departure moved up beside the pair and lost the word "departs", which was doing no work — a route has one date and it is written next to the two airports it belongs to. Cities and the last look go under it, smaller. That frees the second box to hold six figures on a single row, and takes the panel from 237px to 201px. The space between the code and the date is a real character, not the margin that sits there: a margin is not something a screen reader can hear. | 12.44                           | The flight table is scoped to the chart's **most recent bucket of observation time**, and says which one.                                                                                                                                                                                                      | The switch above the chart moves the table with it: day lists the flights seen in the newest day of collection, week the newest ISO week, month the newest calendar month. Observation time rather than flight time, because a watched route has exactly one departure date and bucketing rows by when they fly would drop every flight into one bucket and say nothing. The period is keyed through `buckets.bucketKey`, so the table's week is `isoWeekKey`'s Monday-start week and cannot drift from the line above it — with observations on 16, 17 and 18 August 2026, the Sunday falls outside 2026-W34 and inside 2026-08. Reading the switch this way is an inference, so the caption states the stretch in words, `13 flights seen between 17/08/2026 and 18/08/2026`, where the reader can correct it instead of guessing at it. | 12.45                           | The period narrows **which flights are listed**, never the history behind the Change column.                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Rebuilding the tracks from the period's own snapshots was the other option and it lies at day granularity: `trackFlights` records a price only when it differs from the one before, and a day holds one or two collections, so a fare that had fallen 20% across the week would be reported as never having moved. The same de-duplication is why membership is decided from the snapshots rather than from a track's observations — a flight that sat at $125 all week has its last observation stamped on the first of those days, and deciding from observations drops it from every period after that. Checked by breaking it: the observations version fails three tests, among them "keeps a fare that has sat unmoved all week on the newest day of the board". | 12.46                           | Every column sorts, from a real `<button>` in the header, and **nulls sort last in both directions**.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `aria-sort` sits on the `<th>` so the order is announced as a property of the column rather than as part of its name, and the arrow is `aria-hidden` because it repeats what `aria-sort` already says; a click handler on the cell would have left the whole header out of the tab order. Nulls last is the part worth pinning. A flight with no duration, or with no earlier price to compare against, has nothing to say about the column being sorted, and letting the descending pass float those rows to the top would hand the reader a screen of em dashes and call them the biggest movers. Multiplying the null branch by the direction — the obvious way to write that line — fails two tests. | 12.47                           | Filter options are **read off the rows they filter**, and a row that cannot answer a question fails it.                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Hard-coded options are a way of asking for an empty table: a route flown by two carriers would have been offered twenty, and a domestic hop with nothing over two hours would have been offered "under twelve hours". The airlines, the price range, the stop counts, the durations and the times of day all come out of `facetsOf`, so no single choice can empty the table on its own. The other half of the rule is that a flight with no duration is not "under four hours" and a departure stamp with no clock is not an evening flight — both are dropped rather than quietly passed, and the caption says how many rows went. | 12.48                           | What a flight did is filtered by **five words**, not by a range of percentages.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Rose, fell, unchanged, new and off the board. A numeric band would answer a question nobody asks; these are the questions the archive is kept for. The pair worth separating is new and unchanged, which show the same em dash in the Change column for the same reason: `trackFlights` records a price only when it moves, so a null change means either "has never moved while watched" or "we only met it in this observation". They are told apart by whether the flight's first observation is the newest one on the route. A flight that did move is reported as having moved even when it also appeared this period, because a measured percentage says more than the word new. Changing a filter or the granularity returns to page one, because a different set of rows makes page four meaningless; changing the sort does not, because it is the same rows in a chosen order. None of it is persisted and none of it reaches the chart — a filter still in force a week later is a table lying quietly, and if filtering moved the line then "cheapest" would mean something different depending on which airline was selected. Measured cost: the Airfare chunk grows from 171.39 kB to 179.80 kB (63.99 kB to 66.73 kB gzipped) and its stylesheet from 12.15 kB to 13.57 kB, with 45 new tests behind it. | 12.50                           | The globe follows **mapcn's own palette**, read from its source, and drops the graticule.                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | mapcn's dark theme fills geography with `#404040` and separates it with `#171717` — a line _darker_ than the land it cuts — over a transparent background with no graticule anywhere. Both values are warmed a few percent towards this app's ground so a neutral grey does not read as cold on a brown page; measured after the warming the pair sits at a contrast ratio of 1.70 against mapcn's 1.73, so a border relates to its land here as it does there. The grid of meridians and parallels went with it: on a globe already carrying arcs, airport codes and place names it was the busiest thing on screen and the only one carrying nothing. Borders are drawn as a topojson **mesh**, every boundary once — stroking each country's own outline paints a shared border twice, which reads as a heavier line between France and Germany than along the Atlantic coast. | 12.51                           | Zoom is **continuous, about the cursor, and has no buttons**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Proportional was not enough, and the first attempt proved it. The factor is exponential in the wheel delta (`exp(-deltaY · 0.002)`, d3-zoom's own constant) — a trackpad reports a few pixels per gesture and a mouse notch about a hundred, so a fixed factor per event gives the trackpad a crawl and the mouse a jump — but applying even the right factor _in full, the instant the event arrives_ is a 22% change in one frame, and 22% in one frame is a step. A tile renderer, which is what mapcn is underneath, sets a **target** and animates towards it. So a notch moves the target and the frame loop eases the scale after it: about 63% of the remaining distance per 70ms, framed in elapsed time so the glide takes the same wall-clock time at 60 Hz and at 144 Hz, snapping at the end because an exponential approach never actually arrives and a scale a thousandth away keeps the loop awake for nothing. One step is applied on the event itself, so the map answers a notch in the frame it arrived in. Zoom lives in a ref, not state — a wheel gesture fires dozens of events and each one was a React render before anything could be drawn; held in a ref the handler writes a number and the frame loop picks it up, the same path a drag takes. Scaling about the middle of the frame slides the thing you are pointing at away exactly when you are trying to reach it, so both projections hold the point under the cursor still: the flat map shifts its pan, and the globe _turns_, because an orthographic has no pan. The bug that cost the most: the correction wrote the new rotation _after_ the fit that used it, so every notch drew one frame of un-anchored zoom — the projections are now refitted before `zoomAbout` returns, and a test pins it. Plus and minus buttons are gone; `+` and `-` on the focused map keep zoom reachable without a wheel. | 12.52                           | Airport nodes are a **coloured dot with a white ring**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | mapcn's own marker is a 16px circle with a 2px white border, and the reason it is white rather than the surface colour is that the ring has to separate the dot from land, from ocean, and from an arc of its own colour passing under it — only white is lighter than all three on this ground. | 12.53                           | Investing puts the **watchlist beside the chart** and the positions full width beneath both.                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | The two lists shared one tabbed rail, so reading what you hold meant hiding what you follow. The chart and the watchlist are now two cells of one grid row — the arrangement 12.38 found for Airfare — so whichever has more to say sets the height and the other fills it instead of leaving a ragged gap; only the rows scroll, because a list long enough to scroll is when you least want the search field scrolled away with it. The column minimum is computed, not eyeballed, and the unit matters: this app sets `--font-size-base: 125%`, so a rem is 20px. At 0.6em per character a row needs 300px — 20px of panel padding either side, a fourteen-character name at 0.66rem (127px), a 10px gap, an eight-digit price at 0.78rem (75px) and the 28px remove button — which is the 15rem floor, capped at the 16rem the rail already held. Positions left the column outright: a holding is a row of figures read across. The rows scroll on `contain: size`, not on the `flex: 1 1 0` that was the first attempt and does not work: a growable flex item contributes its whole content to its container's intrinsic height whatever its basis, and a scroll container's max-content size is its content too, so a watchlist longer than the chart grew the row and left the chart panel with a gap under it — the same ragged edge arriving from the other side. Sized as if empty it contributes nothing, the chart alone sets the row, and `flex: 1 1 0` spends the height that leaves. Stacked, where nothing above it has a height to give, the containment comes off and the rows size themselves to a ceiling, or the list would vanish rather than scroll. | 12.54                           | A container query's `rem` is the **root's** rem, so the Investing breakpoint is written in pixels.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | `58rem` was written believing a rem was 16px — 928px. It is not: a font-relative length in a container condition resolves against the root, and this app sets `--font-size-base: 125%`, so it read 1160px. Measured on a 1536px window the usable page is 1141px, nineteen pixels under it, so the query matched, the single-column rule won, and the side-by-side layout 12.53 exists for was unreachable below a window of about 1590px. Nothing failed — jsdom evaluates no container query, so every test passed while the page quietly looked the way it always had. It is 980px now, and derived: 640px of chart, twice the side column because the chart is what the page is for, plus the 20px gap plus the 320px side track. The second query moved from 48rem to the 600px at which the chart's own 28rem floor makes it taller than it is wide. A unit test reads the stylesheet and checks the threshold against the widths it comes from, that being the one thing a rendering test here cannot see. | 12.60                           | A period is the **whole calendar unit**, stated with both its clocks.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | `observationWindow` reported the first and last observation it found inside the bucket, which understated the claim underneath it: a week whose only collection landed on the Tuesday was captioned "on 18/08/2026" while the rows below it were everything the Monday-to-Sunday week had seen. The window is now `buckets.periodBounds` — 00:00 on the first day to 23:59 on the last — so the stretch the reader is told about is exactly the set being counted. `periodBounds` is the inverse of `bucketKey` and is built on the same fact `isoWeekKey` is built on, that 4 January is in week 1, which is what keeps the table's Monday and the chart's Monday the same Monday; a round-trip over all 53 weeks of 2026 pins it. The arithmetic is `Date.UTC` and string splitting throughout, never a local `Date`: `new Date('2026-08-17')` is midnight UTC and prints as the 16th in Lima, which is this page's default origin. | 12.61                           | The chart carries a **snapping crosshair**, and the arrow keys move it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Two hairlines, a tag pinned to each axis, and a readout of everything drawn at that period — band, median, and the provider's baseline when it reaches that far back. It snaps to the nearest period rather than floating between them, which is the only readable behaviour on a series with gaps: a collector that missed two days leaves no bucket there at all, and an interpolated readout would print a price for a day nobody looked at. Ties go to the earlier period so a hand shaking across a midpoint does not flicker. The two series are matched by key and never by index — the provider ships sixty days and our archive starts when the route was added, so a period only one of them reached is reported with the other half as `NO_VALUE` rather than borrowed from a neighbour. The keyboard is not an extra: left and right walk the periods from a focused chart and a `role="status"` region reads each one out, because the rest of this page is keyboard-reachable and a hover-only readout would have made the chart the exception. Drawn into the same SVG as the series for the reason 12.12 gave — on a canvas the price under the pointer would be pixels. Measured cost: the Airfare chunk goes from 180.91 kB to 185.38 kB (67.19 kB to 68.95 kB gzipped) and its stylesheet from 13.43 kB to 14.43 kB, with 31 new tests behind it. | 12.62                           | An axis label's **anchor travels with its plate**, and the price margin is measured from the widest fare.                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Found in a browser, not in a test: the time tag read `7-27` because the plate was placed for a centred label — `x − width/2` — while the label was drawn end-anchored. A `text-anchor: end` on the shared `.tagText` class was overriding the element's own `textAnchor="middle"`, because a CSS declaration beats a presentation attribute whatever its specificity, and the leading `0` landed nine units outside the plate on the dark plot. Measured live: plate 466.9→506.9 against glyphs 457.6→487.1. So `timeAxisTag` now returns `x`, `width`, `textX` and `anchor` as one object and the anchor is applied as a modifier class declared after the base rule, where source order keeps it winning. The same override was silently reversing the first label of the time axis, which asked for `start` and got `end`. Chasing it turned up a second instance from the other direction: the left margin was 62 units, which holds `$139.00` but not the `S/4,580.00` an ordinary long-haul fare prints in the currency of this app's default origin — that needs 70, so its `S` was drawn outside the viewBox. The margin is `marginForPrices` now, sized from `S/12,458.00`, and costs the plot 22 units of width. The width estimate itself held up: 6 units a character predicts 30 for `07-27` and the real `getBBox` is 29.5.                         |
| 12.70                           | The globe's arcs **flow, from each route's origin to its destination**, in CSS — superseding the "nothing animates by itself" half of 12.23.                                                                                                                                                                   | 12.23 bought one thing with its stillness: with nothing moving, the frame loop runs _only_ while a pointer is down, so an idle map costs no script. That property is kept in full — there is still no `requestAnimationFrame` at rest, no component re-renders per frame, and not a line of JavaScript runs while the dashes move. The animation is a declarative `stroke-dashoffset` keyframe on the paths, driven by the browser's own animation timeline. It is honestly _not_ free: `stroke-dashoffset` is not a compositable property, so the browser repaints the arcs each frame — measured at nine routes from the home view, that is nine paths of 694 samples between them, and no layout, no style recalc on anything else and no script. **Direction, proved rather than assumed.** A point at distance `s` along a path sits at pattern coordinate `s + offset`, so a dash is at `s = coordinate − offset` and a _falling_ offset carries it towards the path's end; `greatCircle` samples from `from` to `to`, so the end is the destination. The keyframes therefore travel to −10, and a test checks the first point of a rendered arc against the origin's own dot — the direction is invisible on the symmetric LIM–CUZ and unmissable on LIM–MAD. **One flow across a cut route.** The limb splits an arc (12.41) and only the near stretches are drawn, so `runsFor` measures each stretch's _projected_ length and hands the next one an `animation-delay` shifted by however far along the whole great circle it starts: linear periodic animation, so a shift in time is a shift in distance. Hidden stretches count towards it, which is what stops the pattern sticking to the horizon as the globe turns — measured over 21 airports, a route in this map's home view is almost always one drawn path, but 98 of the pairs tried start behind the limb and their one visible run begins part-way along its own arc. Screen length rather than arc-radians because the projection foreshortens hard near the limb and a phase in radians would bunch the dashes up exactly there. The pattern is 10 units (`5 5`, and `7 3` for the open route, which was `7 4` — eleven units under a ten-unit cycle slides back a unit every time round) and travels one whole period per cycle, so the loop has no seam. That number lives twice — in the stylesheet and as `ARC_DASH_PERIOD` — and is held by a comment on each side rather than by a test: `src` is typed as browser code, so a test reaching for `node:fs` builds under Vitest and fails `tsc -b`, and `?raw` on a `.css` file is intercepted and handed back as a class-name map. Same for the paint order in 12.71 and for the reduced-motion stop. Speed is derived from the prototype rather than copied off it: it moved a `14 10` pattern 33 units a second, one dash length in 0.424s, and 33 units a second under dashes a third the size is a shimmer — holding the dash's own travel constant gives 0.85s a cycle and 0.425s a dash. `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` stops it dead, leaving exactly the static dashed map 12.23 shipped. **Mercator stays solid**, per the second half of 12.41's reasoning: a dash reads as depth on a curved surface and only fragments a line on a flat one, and a solid line cannot show flow — so the flat map shows no direction at all, which is a question for the reader rather than something to change quietly.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| 12.71                           | An airport's ring is painted **outside** its fill, not half over it — refining 12.52 rather than touching the palette.                                                                                                                                                                                         | The complaint was that the colour was not visible enough, and it was not the colour: measured, every entry in `lib/palette` sits at 4.78:1 against the land, 8.74:1 against the ocean and 7.08:1 against its lit side, all seven within 0.02 of each other because the palette is built to one perceived luminance. What was wrong was how much of the dot the reader was getting. An SVG stroke is centred on the shape's edge, so half of a 1.6 ring is painted _inwards_: an `r` of 4 was showing a coloured disc of 3.2, which is 32 square units of colour inside a 72 square unit marker — 44% of the dot. `paint-order: stroke` paints the ring first and the fill over it, so the whole of `r` is colour and the stroke doubles to 3.2 to leave the same 1.6 of ring showing. 50 square units, up 56%, for 0.8 units on the marker's outer radius; the airport codes move from `x + 7` to `x + 9` so they keep the gap they had. The ring still does the separating and it is still white, for 12.52's reason and now with its numbers: 10.60:1 on the land, 19.40:1 on the ocean, 15.71:1 on its lit side, and 2.21:1 against the palette's own worst entry — which is the case that matters, an arc of the dot's own colour passing beneath it, where the fill's contrast against the fill is 1.00 by definition and only the ring can help. The palette files are untouched, and the tests pinning their equal luminance are untouched with them. One number is left standing that a reader should know about: Lima's neutral dot is `--color-text`, and white against it is 1.15:1, so its ring earns nothing — it is kept because the fill alone is 9.20:1 on the land and a second marker rule for one dot is worse than a ring that does no work.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 12.80                           | Positions is a **grid of cards**, and a card's width is derived from the widest line it has to print.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | 12.53 gave this panel the whole page and it spent it on a column of rows: eight holdings put eight short lines down the left of 1101px and nothing beside them, while a portfolio is read by comparing its holdings against each other. Cards now, and the width is derived rather than picked. Berkeley Mono advances 0.6em a character, the estimate 12.53 sized the watchlist with and 12.62 checked against a real `getBBox`, so the widest line a card must print whole — the return, `+12,345.67 · +123.45%`, 21 characters at 0.66rem — is 166px; the holding line is the same 21 characters at worst and already ellipsizes, and the ticker beside its hover actions is only 122px. Padding and border either side make 188px, rounded up to **9.5rem**. The unit is the whole point again: a rem is 20px here, and the same 9.5rem written believing it was 16 would be 152px and a card too narrow for its own return. `repeat(auto-fill, minmax(min(9.5rem, 100%), 1fr))` then counts the columns, because the count is not ours to hold: five across the 1101px panel on the 1141px page 12.54 measured, then four at 900px, three at 700, two at 480 and one at 300, and the `min()` is what makes the last of those shrink rather than overflow. Keeping the two-by-two arrangement inside the card was the first attempt and it doubles the column for nothing, so the four figures stack. The card also grew an edge — side by side, boxes separated only by a hover tint read as one block of text — and the edit form stacks, because the row it replaces is nearer 300px than 190. A unit test reads the compiled stylesheet and checks 9.5rem against the lines it is derived from, jsdom having no opinion about layout; what it cannot prove is the 0.6em advance itself, or that five across is pleasant to read. | 12.81                           | In a grid, **all four arrows move a card one place**, and none of them moves it a row.                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | `useReorder` grew an `axis` option. `vertical` is the default, so the watchlist and the Airfare route list keep exactly the handler and the `aria-keyshortcuts` string they had; Positions passes `both`, which adds Alt+ArrowLeft and Alt+ArrowRight and announces all four. Every one of them means the same thing: one place earlier, one place later. Making up and down move a whole row is the obvious reading and cannot be implemented honestly — how many cards a row holds is `auto-fill`'s answer to the width the panel happens to have, so the offset is three places at one window size and five at another, nothing in the DOM tells the hook which it currently is, and a shortcut whose effect changes when you resize the window is not one anybody can trust. Reading order is stable, and reading order is what gets persisted. Left and right are added because along a row the eye follows the horizontal axis and an arrow pointing at where the next card sits that did nothing would read as a broken control; up and down are kept because a reader arrives having learnt them on the watchlist, and at one column the grid genuinely is a list. Dragging needed no change at all: the drop target is a card instead of a row, and "put `from` where `to` sits" is the same request either way. The cadence in `fare_schedule.due_now` (12.10, 12.17) exists to spend a daily request budget where prices actually move — it is the scheduler's rule, and a person pressing a button on one row has already decided where to spend one request. **No endpoint was added, because the bypass was already there:** `POST /api/fares/collect` calls the collector's unconditional `collect`, never `collect_due`, so it polls whatever it is handed and `skipped` comes back empty by construction. `services/api` is untouched. The row still renders a `skipped` entry if one ever arrives, for 8.8 and 8.41 — a press that quietly does nothing is a broken button as far as the reader is concerned, so every branch says something: what was collected, what was refused and why, or that the pass came back silent. **The affordance is a 12px drawn mark, and the width is arithmetic, not taste.** At `--font-size-base: 125%` a rem is 20px. The panel column is `minmax(20rem, 27rem)`; at its 27rem ceiling that is 540px, less 2px of border and 2 x 20px of panel padding, so 498px of content. `Remove` is six monospace advances of 16 x 0.6 = 57.6px plus 20px padding and 2px border = 79.6px; take it and the row's 10px gap off and the route button has 408.4px, 386.4px inside its own chrome. Its content measures 290.5px — 8px swatch, 7px, the pair at 82.3px, 7px, two date legs at 186.2px — leaving **95.9px**. A second text button needs 99.2px (`Collect` at 67.2px + 22px of chrome + 10px gap): **3.3px more than exists**, against a row the `.dates` comment already records at one pixel of slack before the column was widened. The mark costs 34px, 44px with the gap, and leaves 51.9px standing. It is an inline SVG rather than a `↻` because this repo ships no font file and a glyph the fallback monospace lacks renders as a box; the accessible name is the whole sentence, `Collect LIM → CUZ on 2026-10-17 now`, never a bare "Collect". **Per-row state, in one hook.** Hooks cannot be called in a loop, so `useRouteCollection` keys an in-flight list and a report map by `routeId` and drives one mutation — React Query's `isPending` is one flag for the whole mutation and reading it per row is exactly how one press greys out every other button. The double-press guard is a `useRef` set as well as `disabled`, because only the ref is synchronous: two presses in one tick would both see the old state and both fire an upstream request. A departed route gets no control at all rather than a disabled one — the row already says "Departed", and a greyed button beside it only raises a question.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

Restricting it to the open route makes it say something — this is the row you have selected, and this is the way it goes — and the still arcs keep the dash that reads as depth on a curved surface. Everything 12.70 proved is kept and none of it is re-argued: the direction is still origin → destination by the same `s = coordinate − offset` argument, the phase from `lib/arcFlow` still joins the runs a limb has cut into one line, and the flat map is still solid and still. **The cheap-idle property 12.70 rests on is kept and improved**: still no `requestAnimationFrame` at rest, still not a line of script per frame, and now one repainting path instead of nine — `stroke-dashoffset` is not compositable, so this is measurably less work at rest than 12.70 shipped, and none at all when nothing is selected. `.flow` was already a separate class from `.dashed` so that reduced motion could drop one and keep the other; it now switches on selection as well, which is what that separation was for. **A route with a return date flows on its outbound leg** — which is a clarification, not new geometry: the map has always drawn one arc per route, origin to destination, and a return date adds a second date to the watchlist row rather than a second curve to the globe. Duplicates are dropped on every field we record instead: two rows that agree on all of them are indistinguishable to everything downstream, so collapsing them loses nothing. The arrival joins both identities for the same reason, which is also what keeps the web's and the API's definitions saying the same thing. Ordering is a total order (price, departure, carrier, number, arrival) rather than price alone, because thirteen LIM-CUZ itineraries shared 62.16 and leaving them in arrival order would let Google What changes is the heartbeat beside it. The recipe rides as a `2:digest` prefix on the stored fingerprint rather than as a new field, because the state file is a flat `{flightDate: fingerprint}` map read as `dict[str, str]` by several small Polling all 31 dates on the near rate is 936 requests a day for one month a week out, against a budget of 300; giving the whole month the far rate would poll its near end daily, which is the rate the measurement says is too slow to see anything. Each date is measured against today separately, so a month straddling a cadence boundary runs at two rates at once. `due_now` did not have to change — the collector hands it thirty-one triples where it used to hand it one. Built from an array rather than `toLocaleString`, for the reason the rest of the feature avoids `Date`: handing `2027-03` to a date constructor is how it becomes February in Lima. Measuring presence against it would have marked every flight on the other thirty days as gone. The same reduction gives the detail panel its board: the cheapest departure in the month as that day was last seen, which is the sentence a month watch exists to say and a single date never could. Natural Earth 1:10m, public domain, verified.                                                                                                                                                                               | 12.24 kept the map on 1:110m because 1:50m is 236 kB gzipped, and there is no 1:110m admin-1 in existence: `world-atlas` ships countries and land and nothing else, the only subdivision atlas on npm is `us-atlas`, and Natural Earth's own 1:50m admin-1 turns out to cover **nine countries** — Russia, the United States, India, Indonesia, China, Brazil, Canada, Australia and South Africa — and nobody else. Measured in this repository's `node_modules`: `countries-110m` 108 kB on disk and 39 kB gzipped, `countries-50m` 740 kB, `countries-10m` 3.5 MB. So 1:10m is the only worldwide option and it is 40 MB of GeoJSON, which settles the question — it is Data Plane B, exactly as `app/data/airports.tsv` already is, and for the same reason 12.21 and `airport_search` give: reference data that ships with the code and is served, not bundled. Licence checked at source rather than assumed: "All versions of Natural Earth raster + vector map data found on this website are in the public domain… No permission is needed to use Natural Earth. Crediting the authors is unnecessary." Its own `/api/geography` router rather than another entry under `/api/fares`: an airport is on the archive's list because somebody watched a route through it, and nothing about a province is a fare. **The web bundle does not grow by a geography file.** Measured, the Airfare chunk goes from 188.94 kB to 194.17 kB (70.56 kB to 72.50 kB gzipped) and every byte of that is code — the reader, the hook, the client and `countryAt` — with the main chunk unmoved at 313.67 kB. | 12.121                          | A country's file is its **internal border mesh and its labels**, and the coastline is thrown away.                                                                                                                                                                                                             | 12.50 draws national borders as a mesh so a shared border is painted once; the same argument applied one level down deletes most of the data. Meshing each country's subdivisions with `(a, b) => a !== b` keeps the boundaries _between_ two of them and drops the outer edge, which the map is already drawing at 1:110m — and a second coastline four hundred times finer sitting on the first would not agree with it. It is also where the bytes were: Chile was **6.7 kB** of Andean border where the whole polygon set was 96 kB, almost all of it fjord. **Revised 2026-08-19, when the ceiling went to 32x (12.164): the outline came back, as `land`, and that reversal is the point of the row.** At 8x the bundled 1:110m coast was fifteen pixels to a segment and read as a coast; at 32x it is sixty-one, and a coastline becomes a run of straight lines. The country's own outline is therefore served beside its borders — dissolved out of the very same admin-1 units, in the same topology, over the same arcs. Not from `countries-50m`, which was the obvious move and is wrong twice: 1:50m is a 5 km resolution against 1.03 km to the pixel, and a 1:50m coast under 1:10m provincial borders would not meet them, leaving every coastal province hanging off the edge of its own country. Chile is 124.8 kB again, and that is the fjords being paid for on purpose rather than by accident. With the polygons gone there is nothing left to take a centroid of, so the labels are computed at build time, from the largest piece, by the rule `countries.ts` already uses for the same reason. **Totals: 167 files, 2.12 MB on disk, 744 kB gzipped.** Per country: Peru 21.8 kB, Chile 6.7 kB, Spain 34.5 kB, the United States 40.9 kB, Japan 20.4 kB; the worst in the set is Russia at 170 kB. Simplification is derived, not eyeballed: zoom stops at 8x and the stage is at least 460px on its short side, so the globe's radius never passes 1546px, one screen pixel is 4.1 km of ground, and a Visvalingam threshold of 1e-8 sr leaves the retained vertices averaging **4.0 km** apart — about a pixel at the closest anyone can get. 2e-9 buys 2.8 km spacing that no view can resolve and costs 26% more on disk. For scale, the 1:110m national borders these lines sit inside have a median segment of 63 km.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.122                          | The country under the view **gives its name up to its own subdivisions**, crossing at 4.6x — 12.28's handover, one rung further in.                                                                                                                                                                            | Same shape and the same machinery: a 1.2-wide ramp, the two layers crossing half-lit, `screenArea`, `roomFade` and `withoutOverlaps` unchanged, and the fade coming from the geometry per frame rather than from a stylesheet (12.27). Where it sits is measured. A country must never lose its name before that name has been at full strength, and `roomFade` says when that is — it saturates at twice `LABEL_ROOM`, which on the stage's own 460px minimum lands at 2.0x for Peru, 2.7x for Chile, 3.2x for Spain and **3.7x for Japan**, the binding case among the countries this reader's routes touch. So the fall starts at 4.0, clear of Japan, and the crossover is at 4.6. **The name that goes is only the one country's**, not the layer's: every other country on screen keeps whatever `countryFade` gives it, because no other country's subdivisions have been fetched and withdrawing a name while drawing nothing in its place would leave a blank shape. That is also the whole of the silent fallback — a country Natural Earth does not divide takes exactly the same branch and looks no different from one nobody has zoomed into. **The room rule was one rule for both rungs, and is not any more — revised 2026-08-19, after the first version shipped.** It said “a province too small to write on is too small whichever layer it belongs to”, which is right about a _country_, whose names are all much the same size on screen, and wrong here for two things only measuring found. A flat threshold cannot see how long a word is, so it refused seventeen of Peru's twenty-six departments that could carry their names — 9 named at the cap on a 529×460 stage, three of them under 0.06 opacity — _and_ printed `Aisén del General Carlos Ibáñez del Campo`, 229px of name, at full strength on ground 77px across, straight over its neighbours. The reasoning also missed that by this rung the reader has already asked to see inside one country, so a border with no name on it is the thing they zoomed in to read. `LABEL_ROOM` is untouched and countries still use it; the subdivision rung now sizes its threshold from the name, `roomForName` = the name's own box × 2.7. That 2.7 is not a new number: 2400px² is 2.64 times the box of the mean country name and 3.06 times the median's, so it is the ratio `LABEL_ROOM` already embodied, said out loud instead of averaged over an imaginary name. The face went 9px to 8px with it and the two levers multiply — a name’s box is width times height and both fall with the face, so 11% narrower and 11% shorter is 21% less ground asked of every unit. Measured at the cap: **Peru 9 → 18 of 26**, Chile 5 → 8 of 16, the United States 41 → 42 of 51, Japan 1 of 47 unchanged, **Spain 0 → 2 of 52** — and the four this row first claimed for Spain was estimated rather than measured, which is the other thing the measurement corrected. What is still refused is refused honestly, every one a name wider than its own ground: Peru's remaining eight run from La Libertad (38px of ground, 55px of name) down to Callao at 1.8px, and Spain's fifty are simply five times smaller units than Peru's — 9,700km² against 49,000 — so at the cap a Spanish province is a 25px square and `Guadalajara` is 55px wide. Raising `ZOOM.max` was costed and rejected rather than overlooked: Tumbes is nine times smaller than the smallest department that qualifies, so it needs ~24×, where a pixel is 1.37km and 12.121's own 4.0km vertex spacing shows as three-pixel facets. Both projections, unchanged — `screenArea` already carries the Mercator estimate and the country under the view is one `invert` of the frame's middle either way. Borders are quieter than the national ones in two ways at once, because one was not enough at 8x: 0.55 of the width, on a colour measured at 1.34:1 against the land where the national border is 1.68:1. |
| 12.123                          | Fetching is damped **three ways**, and the third is a cache that never expires.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | A globe being spun must not become a burst of requests, and no one gate does it. **A zoom gate**: nothing is asked for below 3.4x, which is one ramp before the layer is worth drawing — so it is in hand when it is needed and a reader who never closes in sends no request at all, the whole default view included. **A settle gate**: the country under the middle of the frame is only named once the map has been still for 250ms, and a gesture starting again cancels it; the wheel already holds the map "moving" for 320ms past the last notch, so what this quarter second guards is a hand reaching for a second gesture, not the first one finishing. **A permanent cache**: Natural Earth publishes a few times a decade, so `staleTime: Infinity` and a country fetched once is never fetched again in the session — including the ones that came back with nothing, which are remembered as `null` rather than re-asked every time the reader passes over them. The middle of the frame rather than the largest country in view, because that is where a reader puts what they are looking at and it is what the zoom already anchors to; found with `geoContains` behind a bounds test, which throws all but a handful of the 176 shapes out before any point-in-polygon work runs. The 404 is swallowed in `shared/api/geography` and nowhere else: a country with no subdivisions is an answer, and a 500 from our own API is a bug that still has to be heard. | 12.130                          | A watched route may carry an **optional focus date**: one departure inside its month.                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | A month is the right unit to collect and the wrong unit to read. The reader still has one departure they mean to take, and with nothing in the model able to say which, the detail panel, the chart and the flight table could only ever speak about March. Optional and absent by default — every stored route today has none, and a route with none must not look like one that lost something. `routeId` deliberately excludes it, so a focus is a property of a watch rather than a second watch: two entries for one month with different days would collect the same thirty-one departures twice.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.131                          | The focus is applied as a **longer prefix of the same departure key**, not as a second code path.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `readingPrefix` returns `2027-03` or `2027-03-09`, and every reader below narrows on it unchanged: `snapshotsFor` already filtered with `startsWith`, and the history endpoint's `departure` parameter already matched as a prefix — the property 12.112 leaned on to leave the archive alone. So the detail board, the chart, the flight table, the provider baseline and the heartbeat counts all move onto the focused day together, by one string. Nothing anywhere has a `if (focused)` branch that could drift from the heading above it, which is what stops the page naming a day over a month of numbers.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| 12.132                          | A focus outside its own month is **dropped**, and the route is kept.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | The two repairs both invent. Widening the month to the focus's month changes what gets collected, which a reading preference is not allowed to do; moving the day into the watched month names a departure nobody typed. The entry states two things that cannot both be true and does not say which is wrong, so the weaker goes — a route with no focus is a shape the whole feature already handles, because most routes have it. Dropping the route instead would empty a watchlist over a preference. A pre-12.110 `flightDate` is likewise not promoted into a focus: it was the only day that entry could name rather than one chosen out of thirty-one, and a focus everybody has is a focus nobody chose.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| 12.133                          | A focus whose day has **departed is said out loud, not deleted**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | The normalizer takes no clock, and giving it one would mean a document that edits itself because the page was opened a day later. The archive the focus points at is still the archive of the flight the reader meant to take, so it stays; what changes is that every figure under it is frozen at the last look before departure, and a panel that did not say so would read as a live price. Three places say it: the detail panel in words, the collector by reporting the day as `departed` by name, and the add form by refusing a day that has already gone.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| 12.134                          | When the budget truncates a pass, the **focused departure is kept first** — ahead of nearest-first.                                                                                                                                                                                                            | The only reason the focus is stored rather than held in the browser, because the truncation happens on the server and `due_now` is the one thing that knows which departures a pass could not afford. It costs nothing today, and what will change that is the **size of the watchlist, not the calendar**. `budget` is a per-pass ceiling and one pass has exactly as many candidates as there are watched departures — thirty-one per month — so the arithmetic is 300 / 31 and the answer is ten routes. Measured 2026-08-19 by calling `due_now` with every departure due, a budget of 300 and one day focused: at 9 routes (279 departures) the focused day is `due` with the focus and without it; at 10 (310) it is `over-budget` without and `due` with; at 12 (372) the same. Re-run against a `now` in February 2027 those rows are identical — the threshold does not move with the date, because the number of candidates in a pass does not. This row previously argued from the calendar instead, setting the 62, 302 and 2,208 requests two watched months cost **per day** against a **per-pass** cap of 300; the daily figures are right and the comparison was not, and 62 candidates never truncate against 300 however close March 2027 gets. A separate and pre-existing gap, from 12.111 rather than from here: `daily_request_budget()` is spent as that per-pass ceiling and nothing carries spend from one pass to the next, so the day's budget its name promises is not enforced anywhere. Named, not closed. Readiness stays the outer sort key: being kept first is about a truncation, not about skipping the cadence, so a focused day that is not due is still skipped by name.
