                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| 12.135                          | A focus buys **no faster cadence**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Argued rather than assumed, and the measurement behind `DEFAULT_CADENCE_MINUTES` has not changed: a departure 150 days out moved on 22% of days by a median 1.7%, against 27% and 14.0% at 14 days. Starring a date does not change what Google answers about it, so polling a focused far departure every thirty minutes would spend 47 of its 48 daily requests rewriting the same number — and it would spend them out of the same 300 that the focus exists to be protected from. Order is free and rate is not. `poll_minutes` is not told the focus exists.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| 12.140                          | The flight scatter is a **second view, not a replacement**, and one toggle chooses between the two charts.                                                                                                                                                                                                     | The archive has two time axes and they are not interchangeable. `PriceBandChart` is drawn on _observation_ time — what the cheapest fare was on each day we looked — and answers "is this route cheaper than it was". The scatter is drawn on _departure_ time and answers "which departure do I book, and what does the rest of that day's board cost around it". Folding them together would need one x axis to mean both kinds of time at once, and the crosshair of 12.61 would have had to report a band and a flight from the same hairline. So the toggle is the whole of the integration: the price chart is untouched, its crosshair keeps working on its own view, and the day/week/month switch drives both charts and the table underneath so flipping the view never moves the period the reader was on.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.141                          | A dot is **one itinerary on the board of its own departure, as that day was last seen** — never one per collection.                                                                                                                                                                                            | 12.115 applied to a chart. A watched month is polled one departure at a time, so plotting every snapshot ever written would draw the same flight once per look and smear it vertically wherever its fare moved; the board as last seen is one dot per itinerary, which is what the reader drew. It is also the same reduction the detail panel above already uses, so the low point of the cloud is by construction the same flight that panel names as the cheapest departure of the month. A flight whose stamp carries no clock is dropped rather than placed at midnight — there is no honest x for it, and the table below still lists it. Ties for cheapest-of-day go to the earlier departure, because two carriers matching to the dollar is an ordinary morning here and two flagged nodes would make the dashed line double back on itself.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.142                          | Below a week the x axis is a **clock**; at a week and above it is a **calendar with the clock inside each day**.                                                                                                                                                                                               | One rule, three readings. A day is ticked every three hours and carries no date, because there is only one and it is in the caption — what the reader is comparing is the morning against the evening. A week is ticked once per day at that day's midnight, labelled `dd/mm`, because the question has become which _day_ is cheap while the clock still places every dot inside its day. A month is ticked the same way but labelled every fifth day: thirty-one five-glyph labels do not fit in 620 view units and would run together into a grey band, while the faint separator at every midnight still groups the dots so a month reads as thirty-one boards and not as one smear. The `dd/mm` is the one place this feature drops its own `dd/mm/yyyy`, and it is a measurement rather than a preference — the year is stated once in the heading and the window caption instead of seven times on an axis. Every window comes from `buckets.periodBounds`, so a week here starts on the same Monday the table captions.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.143                          | The period is anchored on a **departure day**, and the arrows step to the next period that _holds flights_.                                                                                                                                                                                                    | A month of departures is thirty-one days, so "day" has to mean one of them and something has to choose. An index into the periods was the obvious state and it breaks on the granularity switch: flipping week → day rebuilds the list under it and the index lands on the seventh day of the month rather than on the day being read — the same stale-index failure the price chart's crosshair guards against. A day survives the flip, because both the day and the week containing it derive from it. Stepping moves to the next period with something in it rather than the next calendar unit, and sets the anchor to the first _departure_ day inside it: the Monday of a week can fall outside the watched month, and anchoring on it would break the next flip. An anchor that means nothing for the route now open falls back to the earliest period that does, which is why the component is keyed by route.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.144                          | The scatter gets **its own read-out**; `lib/crosshair.ts` lends its geometry and none of its meaning.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Asked honestly, the answer is half and half. `clampToTrack`, `timeAxisTag`, `priceAxisTag` and `marginForPrices` are about where a plate sits and are reused unchanged, along with 12.62's rule that the anchor travels with the plate as a class. `readingAt` and `readingSentence` are not reusable: they describe a period's band, its median and the provider's baseline, and this chart has no band and no baseline — it has a flight. `nearestBucket` is one-dimensional, which is the whole answer when a period has one value and no answer at all when twenty flights share a day and two share a minute, so the scatter snaps in two dimensions instead. The keyboard follows the same split: left and right walk the dashed line, one press per day, and up and down walk that day's own board by price. Walking all 899 dots in departure order was the alternative and it is not a way anybody crosses a month.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| 12.145                          | **SVG stays**, and the cloud is memoised as elements: 899 dots, 17 ms a pointer move down to 5.                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Measured before deciding, because 899 nodes is where 12.12 would have been worth re-examining. The first render of a full month is 105 to 124 ms in jsdom for 996 DOM nodes, which is a one-off and fine. The cost that mattered was the crosshair: every pointer move sets state, and with the dots rebuilt inline React walks all 899 circles each time — 16.9 to 18.4 ms a move. Held in a `useMemo` on the placed points the element objects are identical across a crosshair-only render and React skips the subtree outright, at 4.3 to 6.1 ms. Both figures are jsdom's, which has no compositor and sets every attribute as a property, so a browser does better than either. That is a `useMemo` rather than a rewrite onto a surface where a flight stops being a node a test can find and a reader can reach. No `<title>` per dot either: 899 more nodes for a native tooltip that waits a second and then argues with the crosshair saying the same thing. Cost on the bundle: the Airfare chunk 189.61 kB to 201.22 kB (70.95 to 74.21 kB gzipped) and its stylesheet 15.35 kB to 18.35 kB, with 65 new tests behind it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| 12.150                          | Every month **outside** the watched one is collected from Google's own calendar graph: one request, one cheapest fare per departure date.                                                                                                                                                                      | 12.110 made a watch a city pair and a month, and left the other eleven months dark. Collecting them as boards is 330 requests a route a day against a budget of 300. `GetCalendarGraph` answers a whole range of departure dates in one request, so the same year costs 2. It carries one number a day and nothing else — no carrier, no times, no itineraries — so it does not replace a board and the search path is untouched. It is a **direct RPC path**, not `batchexecute`: nine earlier tries against `/_/FlightsFrontendUi/data/batchexecute` with rpcid `YMjVO` were answered HTTP 500 every time, right service and wrong transport. The response envelope is batchexecute's, which is what made that plausible.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| 12.151                          | The calendar endpoint needs **no session**, and that was established before anything was built on it.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | The wire format was captured from a signed-in browser, which left the one question that could have sunk the feature: whether the cookies were load-bearing. Measured 2026-08-19 from this repository's own adapter, its own User-Agent and no cookies at all — HTTP 200 with a full 181-date answer. Establishing it first was the point; a store, a collector and an endpoint built on a request that needs a browser session would all have been wasted work.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.152                          | The booking horizon is **two requests per route, not one**, and the width was measured rather than assumed.                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Measured live 2026-08-19 on LIM-CUZ: 21 dates answered, 181 dates answered, and the whole 331-date horizon was refused with gRPC status 3, INVALID_ARGUMENT. A 149-date window whose far end sits 330 days out answered in full, which is what says the refusal was about the **width** of the range and not about how far ahead it reaches. The true cap is therefore somewhere in 182..330 and was deliberately not bisected: it cannot change the design, because two windows already cover the horizon and one cannot. The windows are contiguous rather than overlapping — a departure returned twice would be stored twice under one key and the later answer would silently win.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.153                          | A curve lives in **its own store** under `fares/calendar/`, not as a fifth kind of file in `FareHistory`.                                                                                                                                                                                                      | All four kinds that class owns — snapshot, heartbeat, fingerprint state and provider baseline — are keyed by `(city pair, flightDate)`, and the readers built on that keying include `last_checked()`, which is the board scheduler's own input. A curve is keyed by `(city pair, capturedAt)`: its three hundred departure dates are the payload, not the key. Folding it in would mean either a fifth key shape inside a class whose every reader assumes the fourth, or teaching `last_checked` to skip a kind of row it must never hand the scheduler — a change to the code path that collects the reader's primary data, made for a feature that can be additive instead. The rule is shared and the class is not; the one thing that must never be a second copy, `route_stem`, is imported rather than rewritten. Same append-only, write-on-change, always-a-heartbeat discipline as ADR 0002, and a curve is 7,145 bytes measured on the first real one, so writing on change is what keeps 2.6 MB a route a year a ceiling rather than a floor.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.154                          | A day with **no flights is stored as `null`**, and the window asked for is stored beside the prices.                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Two different absences, and collapsing them would make an unserved Tuesday indistinguishable from a collection that never reached it. A date inside `from`..`to` carrying `null` is a day the provider answered about and had nothing to sell; a date inside `from`..`to` that is missing altogether is a day we never got an answer for. Reading the first as a zero would draw a free flight, and dropping it would lose the distinction entirely. The same reason the heartbeats exist one level down.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| 12.155                          | The calendar's cadence is **one interval — daily — and not the horizon table** of 12.17.                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | 12.17 sets the rate by how far away a departure is, and that rule has nothing to grip on here: one curve spans every distance from today to 330 days out at once, so there is no single `days_out` to look up. The measurement behind the table is what settles it instead — a fare 14 days out moved on 27% of days by a median 14%, one 150 days out on 22% by 1.7%. What this adds is the far months, which move by under 2% a day, while the near month the reader actually watches is already on the half-hourly board cadence. Daily is also the floor `MAX_POLL_MINUTES` already sets. 12.10's pacing is unchanged: sequential, six seconds apart, in the same pass, and the calendar runs **after** the boards so a pass that runs out of goodwill with the upstream loses the cheap thing that repeats tomorrow rather than the only record of what today looked like. Cost: 2 requests per city pair per day, taking the current watchlist from 62 a day to 66.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| 12.160                          | The map details **every country the camera can see**, not the one under the middle of the frame.                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Zoomed into southern Peru at 10x the reader got eleven Peruvian departments named with their borders drawn, Bolivia beside them as a flat shape with only its country name on it, and Chile below the border with no internal lines at all. That was the design working exactly as 12.123 wrote it, and the reasoning was incompletely scoped rather than wrong: "the country they zoomed into" is the right thing to detail and the wrong thing to stop at, because what a reader looks at is a viewport and a viewport is not one country. So the client asks for the set the camera intersects. **Which countries, and in what order, is one sweep.** `countriesInView` samples the frame on a 12px grid through the shown projection's own `invert` -- 44 x 38 = 1,672 points on the reader's 529x460 stage -- and counts which country each lands in, so the count _is_ the on-screen area and one pass answers both halves of the question. A projected bounding box was the obvious cheaper test and is wrong in both directions: Brazil's bounds can contain the whole frame while Brazil is in one corner of it, so a box ranks Brazil first over southern Peru, and a box cannot tell a country that has gone round the limb from one that has not. Going through `invert` is also what makes the flat map need no separate rule -- the globe's clip, Mercator's pan and the antimeridian are already in it. 12px because a country needs about 150px<sup>2</sup> to be sampled at all, a fifteenth of the 2400px<sup>2</sup> `LABEL_ROOM` asks before a country is worth naming, so nothing that could carry a name is missed. Measured at 39ms for the sweep and the plan together, once per settle and never in a frame. **The endpoint did not change and that is the point:** per-country granularity is what makes a fan-out cacheable, since the next view overlaps this one and the countries they share are already in hand. A batch endpoint would re-send them; a bundled file would still be 6.4 MB.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.161                          | A view may spend **256 kB of geometry**, and what it will not stretch to keeps its own name.                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Fanning out needs an answer to how much a view may cost, and the files run from 4 kB to Russia's 618 kB -- the United States alone is eight Bolivias -- so a cap on how _many_ countries is a cap on nothing. It has to be bytes, and bytes have to be known before they are spent, which is what `GET /api/geography/subdivisions` is for: 2.5 kB of index, fetched once, which also removes the requests that were never going to return anything, since a country with no entry has no file and is not asked about. **The number came from the frame, not the wire, and the first attempt at it came from the wire and was wrong by a factor of thirteen.** Argued from the size of the JavaScript this page already downloads, the budget was 1 MB; measured over Europe it fetched seven countries for 974 kB and dragged at **340 ms a frame** against the map's own 26.3 ms. The relation turns out to be flat and simple -- **0.0995 ms of `geoPath` per kB per frame**, twelve countries from 20 kB to 604 kB within a factor of two of that line, because a file's size and its vertex count are the same fact told twice. So 256 kB is 25 ms: the fine layer at its very limit costs about what the whole map already costs, and a drag at its worst is half the frame rate it was. It is also what the view this change exists for weighs -- Peru, Bolivia, Chile and Argentina across their shared corner are 248 kB together. **Order is on-screen area, the middle of the frame first, and a country that will not fit is passed over rather than stopped at.** Stopping was the first rule, on the argument that a prefix of "biggest on screen first" is a rule a reader can watch holding; measured over Europe it left Germany, Italy and Poland coarse for want of the 20 to 50 kB still sitting behind Russia's 604. **The first country is never refused, whatever it weighs** -- the map detailed exactly one country before this and never asked what it cost, so the budget governs the neighbours and the floor under it is what the map already did. **At the limit the map does not pretend.** What is refused is drawn exactly as a country nobody has zoomed into: coarse outline, own name at full strength. Every country on screen is therefore either showing its subdivisions and has given up its name to them, or is showing its name -- there is no third state, so which countries were drawn in detail is readable off the map itself and no banner has to say so. Measured on a 529x460 stage: the tripoint at 32x draws all 3 countries in view for 192 kB; at 10x, 4 of 6 for 248 kB, refusing Brazil and Paraguay at the frame's edge; Europe at 10x draws 5 of 23 and at 4.2x 5 of 43. The honest cost of the cap is the United States and Canada, 842 kB together: standing on that line the United States is detailed as the country under the frame and Canada is not.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |

The neighbours are not: `outlinesOf` now excludes any country that is itself being redrawn, because Bolivia is a neighbour of Peru and painting Bolivia's coarse self back inside the clip would bury the fine Peru-Bolivia frontier under a 1:110m approximation of itself. The border mesh drops an edge when _either_ side is being redrawn, which matters for exactly one edge in the topology -- the frontier between two fine countries, where both tests would have fired. **The tripoint seam 12.165 left open is closed, measured.** That defect was a fine perimeter meeting a coarse neighbour's border: against Bolivia's 1:110m outline, Peru's served frontier sits a median of **7.26 km** away, p90 13.8 km, max 19.8 km -- 7 pixels at 32x and 19 at worst, which is the kink the reader could see. With both countries served, the two files put their shared frontier a median of **0.133 km** apart, p90 0.32 km, max 1.93 km: 0.13 px, 0.31 px and 1.9 px. Peru-Chile 0.110/0.252, Bolivia-Chile 0.126/0.285, France-Germany 0.067/0.246, United States-Canada 0.076/1.337. Fifty-five times closer at the median, and what is left is 12.165's per-country quantization seen from both sides. The frontier is stroked from both files rather than once, which the mesh cannot help since the two topologies share no arcs -- but `--map-border` is opaque and both strokes are the same width, so a line drawn twice a third of a pixel apart is one line. The seam has not left the world, it has moved to the edge of what the camera can see: the fetched set is the visible set, so the fine-against-coarse boundary now falls outside the frame. Confirmed before it was fixed rather than after: on the build without this, `Paraguay` rendered at x=577.5 on a 529px frame, more than half of it past the right-hand edge, because the only cull was against the anchor point being off the frame _by the name's whole width_. `nudgeIntoFrame` clamps the name's box inside the frame and returns nothing when the point itself is off it, which is standard cartographic practice and the same answer `withoutOverlaps` already gives a name blocked by an airport code: a label steps aside rather than vanishing. The step is bounded by construction -- half the name's own width is the whole distance between centred on the edge and flush inside it -- so it stays over the country it names, and a name wider than the frame is centred instead, since an equal amount lost at each end reads as a long name on a narrow map where all of it lost at one end reads as a different word. The subdivision rung looked immune and was not: it was only ever offered the units of the one country under the middle of the frame, which is the one place on the map where nothing is near an edge, and fanning the layer out puts provincial names against the same edge. Measured after the fix at the tripoint: `Moquegua` sits at x=509.4 with a 39.6px box, flush to 529.2, and no name on the frame overflows in either direction. `nameBox` and the tier metrics moved to `lib/globe` with it, because the room test, the overlap test and the edge test all measure a name's box, and three rules that disagree about it are three rules that disagree. Two things broke at once and neither could be left. The subdivision borders were cut for 8x at a 4.0 km spacing — about a pixel then, four pixels now — and the bundled 1:110m national outline, already the weakest thing on the map at a 63 km median segment, went from fifteen pixels to **sixty-one**: a coast drawn as a run of straight lines. No second dataset, no second request, no second cache. **1:10m rather than 1:50m, and the source is the floor, not the target:** measured with no simplification and no quantization at all, raw 1:10m has a mean segment of 2.61 km and a median of 1.62 km — 2.5 and 1.6 pixels at 32x. Quantization stopped being a step _count_ and became a step _size_: one count means 130m for Peru and 1.9 km for Russia, so it is now derived per country from its own extent at 0.005 degrees, and every one of the 167 files lands at a uniform **0.54 px** of rounding. **The swap is the hard part and the reason it is three steps.** Two generalizations of one border never coincide: measured against the served geometry, the bundled outlines of these five countries sit a median of 1.5–5.2 km from it and up to **31 km** at the worst vertex. Drawing the fine shape over the coarse one leaves the coarse one showing past it into the sea; drawing it _instead_ opens a strip of ocean along every frontier where the neighbour's own coarse border was generalised inland. So inside a clip of the coarse shape: put the water back, lay the fine country down, then paint the neighbours' coarse land back over what it does not claim. The border mesh drops every edge touching that country and the fine outline is stroked in its place, so a shared border is still painted exactly once — 12.50, holding at a resolution it was not written for. **Cost:** 2.12 MB to **6.13 MB** on disk, 744 kB to 1.58 MB gzipped; Peru 21.8 to 42.1 kB, Chile 6.7 to 124.8, Spain 34.5 to 63.2, the United States 40.9 to 326.3, Japan 20.4 to 72.9. A label's React key was its own text, and a name is not an identity: forty-eight names in Natural Earth's admin-1 list belong to more than one country and fifteen countries repeat one inside themselves. Misiones is a province of Argentina and a department of Paraguay 237 km away, Amazonas belongs to four countries, La Paz to three, Latvia has two Daugavpils five kilometres apart. One frame holds both of each pair the moment 12.160 fans the layer out across a viewport — before it, only one country's units were ever offered, so the collision could not happen and the key was fine. Two React children under one key is unsupported, and what React 19 does with it is leave one of the two `<text>` nodes behind on the commit that drops it, permanently, because reconciliation has lost track of it. **Measured in Chrome on the reader's own stage:** zoomed to the Argentine-Paraguayan border, one drag left **eight** `Misiones` labels strewn across Brazil and the South Atlantic, none of them near the province, none of them ever moving again — and the console carried one "two children with the same key" warning per commit throughout. The key is now the country and the unit's own index in that country's served file, built in `readSubdivisions` when the country lands rather than in the render, so it is stable across frames and across sessions and does not need the files to carry an id they do not have. After the fix the same gesture leaves none. Everything else on this map that appears, appears by fading, and the fade comes from the geometry rather than from a stylesheet — 12.27 for the names, 12.28 for the handover, `limbFade` and `roomFade` for both — and this was the one layer not using the rule. It cannot be a CSS transition for 12.27's reason and one more: the borders are on the canvas, where there is no node for a stylesheet to hold. So each country carries the moment it landed, and its borders come up while its own name goes down at exactly that rate — at any instant of the arrival the name is at `1 - handover x arrival` and the borders at `inner x arrival`. **The duration is `SETTLE_MS` and the same number on purpose:** the map holds still for a quarter of a second before it asks whose subdivisions to draw, and it gives the answer back over the same quarter of a second, which a reader has already accepted as the map deciding. **It does not take back 12.23's "no frame loop at rest":** the loop this wakes is bounded by construction — the fade is clamped at 1, the last country finishes `ARRIVAL_MS` after it lands, and the tick switches itself off the frame they all have. Under `prefers-reduced-motion` there is no fade at all, read in JavaScript because the stylesheet cannot reach a value computed per frame. **Measured in Chrome, cold, on the reader's own 529x460 stage:** before, the subdivision names went from 0 to 22 in one sample; after, 7 names at 0.24 opacity at 1,370 ms and 20 at full by 1,620 ms — a quarter of a second of arriving where there had been a The first settle after the zoom gate opened therefore planned nothing at all, and the map drew no detail until the index landed and a later settle re-asked. The biggest country on screen is a worse guess than the middle and an incomparably better one than none. **No second settle:** the catalogue was asked for one settle after the gate opened and answered in 4 ms, and the first country was not asked for until **1,041 ms** after that — a whole second settle, waited out because the effect re-runs when the index arrives and starts its timer again from the top, while the view had been still the entire time. Every settle re-ranked the view by on-screen area from scratch, so a country that fitted at one zoom could be outbid at the next by a neighbour that had merely grown, and the reader would watch borders they were already looking at go back to being a blank shape with nothing about that country having changed. The same country, detailed and then coarse again, while the reader did nothing but get closer to it. Nothing was reset on purpose and nothing in the scatter was wrong — the state simply had nowhere to live that outlived the view. The confirmation is that Price history has no period arrows at all: it draws its whole series and pages nothing, so it had no state of its own to lose and nobody had noticed the asymmetry. Under 12.110 that is 1,914 rows the client cannot separate — the same observation date arrives once per departure with a different price each time — and the difference the traveller actually wants, how far ahead of the flight a price was seen, is not computable at all. So `read_baseline` returns a `BaselinePoint` of its own and the wire gains `flightDate`. Deliberately a new type rather than a wider `PricePoint`: the departure is a fact about the _search_ that produced the series, so the parser genuinely does not have it, and widening would have given every parsed point a field that is empty everywhere except after a round trip through the archive. Measured on the one collected route: ARI–SCL holds 1,914 baseline rows over 62 observation dates and 31 departures, every row already carrying its `flightDate`, so nothing had to be backfilled. So `ChartView` gains `lead`, and the observation-time view is untouched: measured in a browser, its bucket counts are still 62 day, 10 week, 3 month, and its readout, legend and axis labels are unchanged. `PriceBandChart` draws both of those views rather than being forked, because the difference between them is entirely words and direction and none of it is geometry: a `BucketAxis` carries the unit, how a key is spelled out, which way the axis runs, and the legend line for the provider's series. A copy would have meant a second crosshair, and both bugs this chart has had — 12.61's stale index and 12.62's tag anchor — were in the crosshair. One thing the sharing costs, found in a browser and not by a test: two views of the same component type keep one React instance, so the crosshair index followed the reader from the price history onto the lead axis and the walk started at 281 days ahead instead of 284. A key per view, which is what the change of component type used to do for free. | 12.173                          | On the lead axis Day, Week and Month are **1, 7 and 30 days before departure**, and every label says so.                                                                                                                                                                                                       | Calendar buckets are meaningless here — the unit is days until departure, and a Monday has nothing to do with it. Seven because a week of lead time is how people actually talk about booking, and because it keeps the bucket count comparable with the calendar view. Thirty rather than twenty-eight because "a month ahead" means about thirty days, where four weeks is a different claim wearing the same word; the price is that lead weeks do not nest inside lead months, which calendar weeks do not do either. Every label carries its unit — `194d ahead`, `189–195d ahead`, `180–209d ahead` — so that none of them can be read as a date on a page whose other charts tick `09/03`, and the readout spells it out again as "189 to 195 days before departure". The whole bucket is named even where only part of it was observed, which is 12.60's rule carried across. The axis runs furthest-ahead on the left to the day of departure on the right, because left to right is time running forwards on every other chart here; that means the keys are drawn in _descending_ order, which is why `BucketAxis` carries a comparator. Measured in a browser on ARI–SCL: 91 lead days, 14 lead weeks, 4 lead months, spanning 284 down to 194 days ahead. What the axis does change is what the baseline is worth — 60 of its 91 buckets are the only evidence there is about buying six months out, and will stay that way until the collector has run for six months. For the buckets we have not reached, the readout already said "nothing of our own observed"; the drawing did not, so `contiguousRuns` splits either series into runs of neighbouring buckets and the band and the median line become one subpath per run. On today's data neither view changes shape — our lead days are a contiguous 31 and the provider's are dense — which is the point: the lie was latent on both axes and would have appeared the first day the collector missed. A reader has an answer to "which day are you flying"; they do not have a second, independent answer to "which month", and asking both was asking one question twice. `monthOf` derives the watch from the date. **Nothing about collection changes**: the month is still what the collector expands and all thirty-one of its departures are still polled. The date becomes the focus and drives exactly what a focus already drove — `readingPrefix`, the detail panel, the chart, the flight table, and the place at the front of the truncation queue (12.134). So every newly added route carries a focus, and 12.130's objection to that — "a focus everybody has is a focus nobody chose" — is answered rather than ignored: it was aimed at _inventing_ a focus from a legacy `flightDate` on the reader's behalf, and a date typed into a field labelled Departure date is the opposite of an invention. What that objection still forbids is untouched: no stored route is given a focus it did not choose, and the normalizer still takes no clock and still edits nothing by itself (12.133). Deriving it at each is three chances to disagree about something the document can simply state. The other direction is ruled out outright: a model holding only a date cannot represent a route stored before 12.180, which has a month and no day, and those must keep working without having a day invented for them. So `focusDate` stays optional in the type even though the form now always sets it — the optionality is what a legacy route and a cleared focus both need, and both still occur. The invariant that a focus falls inside its own month is unchanged and still enforced in two places, the normalizer (12.132) and `setFocus`.
