| 12.182                          | Clearing a focus is the **detail panel's job alone**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | 12.130 gave the form one control that did both: re-adding a watch with a day set the focus, re-adding it without one took the focus off. It is not replaced by a second control on the form: an "or watch the whole month" checkbox beside a date input is a second independently editable value in exactly the shape 12.185 exists to keep out. The way back is where it already was and where it belongs — "Read the whole month" in the detail panel, which is the panel a focus changes, one click from any row. `setFocus(…, null)` is untouched, so the transition did not change; only the control that reaches it did. `addRoute` still passes `focusDate ?? null` through, because it is a pure transition over a value and making a case unreachable in the UI is not a reason to let the function invent a focus instead. | 12.183                          | The watchlist row still shows the **month**, not the date the reader picked.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Re-examined rather than carried over, because the reader now always picks a date. The argument holds and is stronger than it was: the row carries the collect control, that control polls thirty-one days, and one date printed beside it would claim otherwise. The row is the collection list; the focus is a reading preference, and the detail panel names the picked day the moment the row is selected. `routeId` excludes the focus, so a row is never ambiguous between two watches of the same month. The reader did not ask for the row to change. Nothing was re-measured because nothing on the row moved: at the panel's 27rem ceiling it still has 148.5px spare after the collect mark, and at the 20rem floor it is still 44px over and scrolling — which is also why adding a `dd/mm/yyyy` beside `March 2027` would have spent width the floor does not have. | 12.184                          | A departure past the **collectable horizon is refused in the form**, naming the last day that works.                                                                                                                                                                                                           | 330 days, the figure `MAX_DEPARTURE_HORIZON_DAYS` was measured at: +330 returned itineraries, +340 and beyond returned an error. Until now the form took any future month, and one past the horizon collected nothing forever while reporting `beyond-horizon` once per departure per pass into a skip list the reader has no reason to open — a refusal arriving where nobody can connect it to the thing they typed. The constant is copied to the web side rather than fetched: there is no config endpoint to ask, and adding one would put a round trip on every page load in front of a single integer that has moved once. The server stays the authority, so the copy can only ever offer a refusal earlier — never grant a permission wrongly, since a stored route past the horizon still skips by name whatever the form believes. Both ends are declared on the control as `min` and `max` and both are repeated in the submit guard, because a browser that degrades `type="date"` to a text box enforces neither. | 12.185                          | The month and the focus are made **unable to disagree**, rather than guarded against disagreeing.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | The two-control form had exactly one state its invariant could not survive: a day left standing when the month moved out from under it. It was guarded by clearing the day on every month change, and the guard earned its place because the break was **silent** — `type="date"` renders `09/03/2027` identically whichever month is picked above it, so a reader looking straight at the form could not see the two fields disagreeing, and `readingPrefix` would then narrow the whole page onto an empty set under a heading still saying March. One field removes the second value rather than defending it: the month is computed at submit from the date being submitted, so there is nothing that can be stale. The guard goes and the reason it existed is kept — whatever this form grows next, the month and the focus must never again be two independently editable values, because a date control cannot show which month it belongs to and the disagreement would go unseen. | 12.190                          | The horizon is **a fourth view in the same switch**, and **its own component** rather than a fourth `BucketAxis` on `PriceBandChart`. The placement is **provisional**.                                                                                                                                        | Two decisions, and the second was checked against #88's chart rather than assumed. `PriceBandChart` builds its x axis from the union of the keys its two series carry, so a departure date with no price is simply not on the axis and the gap closes up — the spacing stops being a calendar, which is the one property this chart exists to have. It also has no second series to put behind a curve that has no baseline, nowhere to name two different kinds of absence, and a `<title>` per bucket, which is 331 of them here. So the arithmetic is shared and the component is not: `summarise` builds the bands, `spanOf` scales them, `contiguousRuns` splits them, `lib/crosshair.ts` places every tag. Placement is one entry in `VIEWS`, one in `WHAT`, one branch in the ternary and two props — nothing calendar-shaped anywhere else in the panel — because the owner's decision was to see afterwards whether it gets combined, and a view that costs four lines to mount costs four lines to move. | 12.191                          | The horizontal axis is **which departure date**, spanning the curve's own `fromDate`..`toDate`, and the figure says so **in words**.                                                                                                                                                                           | This is the page's **fourth** meaning of a time-shaped x since 12.172 — history plots when we looked, lead time plots how long before the flight we looked, the scatter plots when the plane leaves inside a period, and this plots which day you fly. Four is well past what a reader can infer from tick labels, so the caption names all four rather than only the ones that existed when it was written. So the caption states both axes as prose above the key, and the two ends of the domain are written out under the plot as `19/08/2026` and `15/07/2027` rather than left to the crosshair. The domain comes from the row and never from today's clock: a curve is written only when something moved, so the one on screen may be weeks old, and deriving the window from `Date.now()` would slide the whole horizon a day every midnight and put every price against the wrong departure. The collection stamp is printed beside the count for the same reason — `Collected 19/08/2026 15:49 from google-flights. 331 departure dates drawn.` | 12.192                          | Day / Week / Month mean **331 / 48 / 12 periods** over the horizon, and there are **no previous-next arrows** at all.                                                                                                                                                                                          | Measured on the real ARI→SCL curve, 2026-08-19 to 2027-07-15: 331 departure dates, 48 ISO weeks (2026-W34 to 2027-W28), 12 months. A coarser period is a band over the departure dates inside it — cheapest, dearest and the median of them — which is literally a `Bucket` — built by `buckets.summarise`, scaled by `spanOf` and split by `contiguousRuns`, all unchanged — so nothing here re-derives a median and a week cannot mean one stretch on one chart and another on the next. `contiguousRuns` is handed **every** period in the window rather than only the priced ones, which is what makes an empty departure date a hole here at all; on the other charts a period neither series reached is simply not on the axis. What is _not_ shared is paging: the scatter's arrows exist because it draws one period at a time out of a month, and this draws the entire horizon in one frame, so an arrow would have nothing to step to. The arrow keys move the crosshair one period and the screen-reader help says outright that there is no next period. | 12.193                          | A calendar point is **not** `readingAt`'s band-and-baseline, and `lib/calendarCurve.ts` says what it is instead.                                                                                                                                                                                               | `crosshair.readingAt` describes two series over one observation stamp, one of which is context for the other; its `baseline` field would be a permanent `—` here, and the facts this chart must report — how many of a period's departure dates were priced, how many had nothing on sale, how many were never answered for — have nowhere to go in that shape. The geometry is shared to the last unit: `clampToTrack`, `timeAxisTag`, `priceAxisTag`, `marginForPrices`, `tagWidth`, `WIDEST_PRICE_LABEL`, `labelSpan` and `tagHoldsLabel` all come from `lib/crosshair.ts`, so the tags, insets and price margin match the other charts exactly. Same split 12.144 made for the scatter: lend the geometry, not the meaning. `periodBounds` is likewise not reused — a horizon's first and last periods are partial, so a period is named by the departure dates it actually covers (`departing 19/08/2026 to 31/08/2026`, not the whole of August) and carries no `00:00`/`23:59` clock, which belongs on an observation stamp and not on a date you fly. | 12.194                          | `nearestBucket` **is** the right snap here, unlike on the scatter, because a curve is genuinely one-dimensional.                                                                                                                                                                                               | `FlightScatterChart` deliberately did not use it: twenty itineraries share a departure day and two share a minute, so an x alone cannot name one and the snap had to work in two dimensions. A calendar curve is one price per departure date with no two periods at the same x, which is exactly `nearestBucket`'s contract — an array of strictly increasing positions in, an index out, strictly-nearer-wins so a pointer on a midpoint does not flicker. Reimplementing a snap here would have been a reimplementation of nothing. | 12.195                          | **A day with nothing on sale and a day nobody asked about are drawn as two different marks**, and a line is never drawn across either.                                                                                                                                                                         | 12.154 goes to the trouble of storing the difference and the last step is where it would be thrown away. Every period between `fromDate` and `toDate` is emitted whether or not it holds a price, because dropping the empty ones closes the gap and slides every later departure onto a day it does not belong to — the axis stops being a calendar. A period with no price gets no point on the plot and a mark on a rail under the plot floor instead, never inside it, since a mark at any height inside the plot is a price: a **filled square** for an answer that came back empty, a **hollow ring** for an answer that never came. The curve breaks at both, because a line drawn across either quotes a fare nobody quoted, which is worse than a gap for looking like data. The crosshair says which in words — "nothing on sale" against "no answer collected" — and a period holding both is counted both ways and drawn as the ring, the weaker of the two claims.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.196                          | **Dots appear when there is room for a dot**, by spacing arithmetic rather than by a granularity table.                                                                                                                                                                                                        | 331 departure dates across the plot's 660 view units is one every two, so a marker per date would draw a six-unit-wide caterpillar over the line that already carries the shape. The rule is a floor of 8 units of track per dot: day gets none, week (14 units apart) and month (60) get one each, and a priced period stranded between two empty ones gets one at any density, or it would vanish — a run of one cannot be a line. Measured in the browser on the real curve: 13 SVG nodes at day, 158 at week, 50 at month; bucketing all 331 dates and splitting them into runs costs 0.77 ms at day, 1.10 ms at week and 0.46 ms at month (mean of 200 runs, dev build, re-measured after the move onto `summarise`/`contiguousRuns`); a pointer move commits in a median 2 ms and a worst 4.8 ms. The band is skipped entirely where no period can hold a spread, which at day is all of them. | 12.197                          | **Flights and Departure dates are one axis at two scales**, and four peers in one switch is the weak seam in this design. Recorded, not acted on.                                                                                                                                                              | Price history and lead time are genuinely different clocks — when we looked, against how long before the flight we looked. The other two are not: both are _which departure_, one inside the watched month at the resolution of a single itinerary's departure time, the other across all 331 days at the resolution of a date. A switch that presents them as peers asks the reader to choose between two zoom levels as though they were two questions. The shape this may want is one Departures view whose granularity keeps zooming out past a month — which would also retire the `aria-label` problem, since "Group observations by" would then be describing one thing. It is written down rather than built: merging them is a design change nobody asked for, and the calendar's placement is itself provisional (12.190), so the merge would be arguing about a room the furniture may yet leave.                                                                                                                                                       |
| 12.198                          | The curve is fetched **beside the archive on the page**, not inside the panel that draws it, and it is **not gated on the open view**.                                                                                                                                                                         | The first version took an `enabled` flag so a reader who never opened the chart never paid for the 15 kB. Measured on the real ARI–SCL watch, the archive this page already fetches for every route it opens is **144 kB** — the curve is under a tenth of what is already being spent, and gating the smaller of the two is not a saving worth structural cost. The cost was structural: `view` lives in `AnalysisPanel` since 12.170, so only that component knows whether the chart is open, and putting the query there turned a component that is handed all its data into one that needs a `QueryClient` — ten of its tests broke on the provider alone. The query sits beside `useFareHistory` instead, keyed by the city pair rather than the watched month because a curve spans every month at once and two watches on one pair are one row. | 12.200                          | The analysis head names **what its figures are of** — the focus date where there is one, the month where there is not.                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.201                          | The analysis panel offers **two charts, not four**: how one flight's price moved, and what each departure day costs.                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| 12.202                          | Observation time and lead time are **one chart with a labelling control**, not two views. Both series stay on both readings.                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.203                          | Flights and the booking horizon are **one axis with a zoom** — the watched month at the near end, 331 departure dates at the far end.                                                                                                                                                                          |
| 12.204                          | The run-up reading is offered **only where the route names one departure date**, and its absence is explained rather than silent.                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.205                          | The granularity switch is named **"How much time one point covers"**, because it has not grouped observations alone since 12.170.                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.210                          | A manual collection **runs on the server and is asked about**, rather than holding the browser open until it finishes. `POST /api/fares/collect` answers 202 and a progress document; `GET /api/fares/collect` answers the same document until the pass ends. The forty-request per-call ceiling goes with it. | The ceiling was never a judgement about the upstream: forty was the browser's five-minute `fetch` deadline divided by the six-second gap. Under 12.110 the owner's two watched months expand to sixty-two departures, so a full refresh could not fit in one press and never could — it took two, four minutes apart, with a person in between who had to know that. Nothing about the upstream had to change to fix it, which is why this was done before the pacing was touched: same order, same schedule, same one-at-a-time loop. Measured end to end against the live provider, the press now returns in **6.7 ms** where it used to block for the length of the pass, and what bounds a pass is `daily_request_budget()` — which is what the bound should always have been. One pass runs at a time and a second press is answered with the running one, because the gap paces a single loop and two loops would halve it with nobody deciding to; that was already the honest behaviour and was previously enforced nowhere, so two rows pressed together really did run two loops. `watching` is how a row tells its own pass from somebody else's, and `polling` is settled before the first request so a four-minute pass has a denominator from the start rather than a spinner. Note, unfixed and predating this, that the budget is spent per pass and nothing accumulates spend across passes, so the daily figure the name promises is not actually enforced.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| 12.211                          | The gap between upstream requests goes from **six seconds to three**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Six was never measured. Timing a real pass put **86.5% of its wall clock inside that sleep**: ten paced requests took 62.5s, of which 54.1s was sleeping, 6.3s was the upstream and 0.03s was parsing. Sleeping _is_ the cost of a manual retrieval and everything else is rounding, so the gap is the only number that moves it. Three is what was measured rather than what was hoped for: twelve requests at three seconds and twelve more at two came back clean — no 429, no consent redirect, no `ErrorResponse` — and mean latency **fell** as the gap closed (0.630s at six, 0.421s at three, 0.394s at two), which is a warm connection and the opposite of a throttle. Two was clean too and is deliberately not taken; three is the pace with the most evidence behind it and the unused floor is the margin. What this does not rest on is daily volume, which nobody here has probed — and does not have to, because pacing changes how tightly a day's requests are packed and not how many there are. A watchlist spending 66 requests a day at six spends the same 66 at three. Together with 12.210 a full manual refresh goes from **401s across two presses to 224s across one**, unattended.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| 12.212                          | **Open, with a recommendation, deliberately not decided here: should a manual press be able to override the cadence?** Recommendation — keep the cadence as the default and add an explicit, separately-labelled force, rather than making the ordinary press ignore it.                                       | The complaint is real and reproduced: pressing Collect at 21:04 having collected at 14:41 returns 31 × `not-due`, and that is 12.111 working exactly as designed. Two things pull against each other. A press is now cheap to _start_ and returns instantly, which makes it easier to press and therefore more dangerous, not less: one press is sixty-two requests, a fifth of the day's budget, and a control that spends that per click with no wait to discourage a second click is precisely what the cadence exists to prevent. Against that, the reader who presses has almost always decided they do not believe the last look, and arguing with them is what makes a button feel broken — the row saying `31 not-due` is a mitigation, not an answer. The recommendation splits them: the ordinary press keeps the schedule, and a deliberate act — a modifier, or a second control that names what it does — runs a pass that ignores `not-due` while still respecting the request budget. That way the default protects the budget and the override is available to someone who has decided to spend it. Not implemented: which of those it should be is a question about the control, and the owner has not been asked.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| 12.213                          | `merge_airports` **writes nothing when nothing is new**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | It runs once per upstream request and the table it maintains has one row per airport ever seen, so after a route's first look every later call rewrote the identical handful of entries. The median cost is 2.8ms, which is nothing, and is not why this changed: the write goes through a temporary file and a rename, and those two syscalls were seen taking **1.6s and 4.9s** inside a live pass on the owner's machine — 15% of one measured run's wall clock, all of it in two stalls. A zero return was already what the old code gave, _after_ writing; the test therefore leaves a mark in the file and asserts it survives, because a count proves nothing about a write. An airport whose details genuinely changed is still written, or the saving would have been bought by dropping data.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| 12.214                          | **What was measured and left alone**: concurrent upstream requests, the calendar's shape as a substitute for boards, and caching the parse.                                                                                                                                                                    | _Concurrency_ is the obvious way to make 224s smaller and is refused for the reason 12.10 gave and measurement did not overturn: sequential-with-a-gap is the whole of what paces this repository against an unmetered upstream, and parallel requests would spend an address to save minutes on a control nobody watches any more now that it runs in the background. _The calendar_ answers 331 departure dates in 2 requests and 51 kB, against a board's 1 date in 1 request and **2.0 MB** — 165 times cheaper per date, and measured here rather than assumed. It still cannot carry the boards: it is one rounded cheapest number per date with no carrier, no times and no itineraries, so riding it would change what is collected and what the archive holds, which is out of scope and would be the wrong trade anyway. Using it as a _filter_ — re-fetching only boards whose calendar price moved — is the tempting version and is refused on correctness: a cheapest fare can hold still while the board underneath it changes, so the archive would silently miss changes it exists to record. _Parsing_ was measured at **3ms against a 2 MB response**, so fingerprinting a response to skip a re-parse would save three milliseconds and cost a cache; it is not worth a line of code.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 12.220                          | `durationMinutes` **means the whole journey** — departure to arrival, layovers included — and **keeps its name**.                                                                                                                                                                                              | Two honest definitions existed and only one of them is what the column is headed with. The field was the legs' flying time with the ground between them discarded, which is right for a non-stop and wrong for everything else: on the LIM-SCL board for 2027-01-21, LA 2127 changes plane at Cusco and read **280 minutes, "4h 40m", against a real 455**, sorting above the 205-minute LA 2697 non-stop beside it in a column a reader picks a flight on. The alternative was to keep air time and rename the field so nobody read it as the journey, which was rejected on two counts: nobody chooses an itinerary on time in the air, and the rename would have to reach the flight table, the scatter tooltip and the duration filter — a column heading in `apps/web` that is already correct, for a number that would then need a second field to answer the question the first one stopped answering. The meaning moved instead of the name, so no consumer changed. A non-stop is unaffected by construction: with no layover the two definitions are the same number, and both LIM-SCL fixtures and all 103 archived ARI-SCL rows still read exactly as before.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| 12.221                          | The journey is **read from the provider**, at itinerary position 9, with legs-plus-layovers arithmetic kept as the **proof that position still means it**.                                                                                                                                                     | Google states the total itself, beside the leg list — the number its own duration column prints. Measured 2026-08-19 across all 68 itineraries in the four captured boards, it equals the legs' flying time plus the ground between them, every time. Reading it is an observation where summing would be a derivation, which is the distinction this whole fix is about. But an integer at a position is exactly the drift this adapter's docstring warns about, and a wrong duration is not absurd on its face the way a wrong airport code is, so the arithmetic is not thrown away: `_journey_minutes` recomputes it on every parse and raises `parse-drift` when the two disagree. A board where **no** itinerary carries a duration is drift too — decision 12.4, for the one field whose absence is an empty column rather than a missing row.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.222                          | The journey is **not** `arrivalAt - departureAt`, however free that looks.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | Both stamps are local wall clock at their own airport with no offset attached — `FareOffer` says so deliberately — so their difference is the journey plus however far the destination's clock is from the origin's. On LA 2127 it reads 07:10 to 16:45 as **575 minutes, "9h 35m"**, overstating the real 455 by exactly the two hours between Peru and Chile in January. Understating by 175 and overstating by 120 are the same kind of wrong. The one interval those stamps _can_ measure is the layover, because a connection lands and leaves at a single airport on a single clock — which is what makes 12.221's cross-check exact rather than approximate.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| 12.223                          | **"Direct" is established, not assumed**: the legs must start where the itinerary starts, finish where it finishes, and join end to end, or the payload is drift.                                                                                                                                              | `transfers` was `len(legs) - 1`, which made the leg list the only witness to the strongest claim the flight table makes, and the parser could not tell "the response said one leg" from "the response gave me nothing I could read as legs". Measured, not argued: truncating the two-leg AV 74 / AV 46 itinerary in the LIM-MAD capture to either one of its legs produced an offer reading `transfers=0` with an arrival time that looked entirely plausible because it was Bogota's, and flattening the two legs into one did the same. So a parse failure really could read as "Direct", and decision 12.4 makes that a typed error rather than an answer. The check is free — the itinerary states its own endpoints at positions 3 and 6, and so does every leg. Unreadable and contradictory are kept apart: codes that cannot be read are one bad row the board survives, codes that read clearly and disagree are the layout having moved and leave the parser.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| 12.230                          | The scatter's cheapest-of-day line **breaks at every departure day with no cheapest flight**, through `contiguousRuns` widened to split any keyed series.                                                                                                                                                      | This was the page's one true synthesised continuity. The line emitted a single `M…L…L…` through whatever days carried a dot, so a week collected on the Monday and the Sunday drew as a straight run between them — five departure days nobody has ever priced, rendered as a fare gliding evenly across them, and indistinguishable from a one-day step because a dashed line looks the same either way. Both reasons a day can be empty break it and neither is guessed at here: a day nobody collected and a day whose board came back empty are both days with no cheapest flight. The answer is the sibling chart's rather than a second one — `contiguousRuns` was already the feature's single answer to run-splitting, and widening its constraint from `Bucket` to "anything with a key" left every existing caller untouched. The axis it measures against is every date the window covers. **The assertion has to measure segment length, not count subpaths:** a single stroke straight through a hole is one `M` exactly like a single stroke across a step, which is how this survived the tests it already had.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| 12.231                          | The price chart's x axis is a **measure in days**, not a list of keys — `BucketAxis.position`, and enough labels to read a distance off.                                                                                                                                                                       | Buckets were spaced by their index in the sorted key list, so a one-day step and a fortnight the collector was down were the same width, and a period neither series reached had no width at all to be seen in. With two tick labels on the whole axis nothing revealed it. Position is whole days on both readings, which is what lets one component keep drawing both: a calendar key sits at the first day of what it covers, a lead key at minus its far end, because that axis counts down while every axis in this app runs forwards left to right. Labels are emitted by spacing rather than every nth key — with keys spaced by time, every nth key is two labels on top of each other in one place and none in the next. This is also what lets the observation chart name the absence it cannot mark: nothing in the archive records a day we meant to look at and did not, and a blank stretch is now as wide as it really was.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| 12.232                          | A board that came back **empty is counted, not dropped**, and marked on a rail below the plot floor on all three charts.                                                                                                                                                                                       | `bucketSnapshots` skips a snapshot with no offers, and rightly — zero is a price and a chart would draw it as the best deal ever found. What went with it was the fact that we asked. A day the provider answered about and had nothing to sell came out of that filter identical to a day nobody ever looked at, everywhere except the horizon chart, which is the one view the store goes to the trouble of keeping the distinction for. The mark is the horizon chart's, unit for unit: a filled square for answered-and-empty, a hollow ring for never-answered, both under the plot floor because a mark inside the plot at any height reads as a fare. The observation chart draws only the square — there is no record of a period we meant to observe and did not, so the ring has nothing to stand for there and 12.231's metric axis says it instead. The crosshair resolves over an unsold period rather than vanishing: it is on the axis because we looked, and a chart that drew the mark and then declined to say what it meant would be worse than one that never drew it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.233                          | Every y-axis tick is a **round number inside the padded band**, never the padded band's own ends.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | All three charts pad the vertical domain before drawing — a fare series hard against the frame is unreadable — and all three printed the padded ends and their midpoint as the axis labels. On the owner's real horizon that is $22.63, $118.79 and $214.94 against data running $41.24 to $196.33: three figures nobody was ever quoted, set in the same money format as the quotes. Two ways out were on the table and ticking at observed values is not one of them — the midpoint of two observations is not an observation either, and a three-tick axis of real quotes would have to be three specific quotes chosen for their height, which is a stranger claim than a scale. So the axis reads as a scale: $50, $100, $150, $200 cannot be mistaken for a fare somebody paid. The step is a power of ten times 1, 2, 2½ or 5, chosen by trying them all and keeping the count nearest four rather than by the usual log-distance rule, because these bands are narrow and that rule hands 612–748 a step of 50, which is two gridlines on a whole chart. The real extremes moved into words: every chart's accessible name now states the observed span, where two of them stated the frame.                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 12.234                          | Where a period has **no figure to sit on, the keyboard crosshair draws no horizontal hairline and no price plate**. Under a pointer both stay.                                                                                                                                                                 | The fallback was the middle of the padded domain, and the plate over it printed that as currency — so arrowing onto a departure date with nothing on sale drew `$118.79` on a tag beside a readout saying `—` and a live region saying "no fare", a number invented twice over by the padding term and then by halving it, in the one visual element on the chart that looks like a quoted price. The plate was already `aria-hidden`, so this was never wrong for a screen reader; it was wrong for everybody else, and "the sighted reading is the broken one" is not a reason to leave it. The pointer case is kept deliberately and is not the same claim: there the height is the reader's own and the plate reads as the ruler they are holding, which is what makes it usable for "is this above or below what I paid".                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| 12.235                          | The scatter's frame is **clipped to the departure dates the route is a watch on**, not to the whole calendar period.                                                                                                                                                                                           | March 2027's last ISO week runs 29 March to 4 April, so at Week granularity the month zoom drew ticks and day separators for the 1st to the 4th of April and captioned itself "between 29/03/2027 and 04/04/2027" under a heading reading "departing in March 2027". No dots were ever placed there and none could be — but the frame asserted four departure dates the watch has never had anything to do with, which is a line across a hole drawn in axis furniture instead of in a path. The clip is the _watched_ range and not the days that happen to carry flights, which is 12.232's distinction: a day inside the month whose board came back empty is a day this chart must draw and mark, not one it may quietly crop away. It comes through `periodBounds` like everything else about calendars in this feature, so the frame and the caption cannot disagree about which Monday.
