                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| 12.236                          | The horizon chart marks a **partly-priced period too**, and names the absence in its tooltip beside the price.                                                                                                                                                                                                 | The mark was drawn only where a period held no price at all, which at `day` is every hole there can be and at `week` or `month` is a minority of them. A week of seven departure dates with two never answered for drew as an ordinary connected point on an unbroken curve: the band and the median were honest about the five and silent about the two, and silence is what this chart is built to avoid. The mark is now about the absence rather than about the emptiness. "The median of this week is $118" and "the median of the five days of this week we have a price for is $118" are different claims, and the tooltip is where a reader asks which. | 12.237                          | A **failed** `/api/fares/calendar` is reported as a failure, not as a route nobody has collected.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | `isPending` is false on a failed query and `isError` was never surfaced, so a 500 rendered as "No booking horizon collected for this route yet." — a fact about us reported as a fact about fares, and the worse half of it is that it tells the reader the collector has never run when what happened is that we could not ask. Three states now travel from the page rather than one: in flight, failed, and answered-with-nothing-on-disk. `role="alert"` on the failure, because a request that fell over while the reader was looking at the panel is news rather than description. | 12.240                          | The analysis panel keeps **two charts and no modes**: the labelling toggle and the zoom both go, and neither is replaced.                                                                                                                                                                                      | 12.201 cut four views to two and left two switches behind them — how to read chart A's axis, how far out chart B stood. Both were the page asking the reader to answer something it already knew. Chart A's two labellings were one series drawn twice (12.241); chart B's two ends were two archives, and which one holds a departure date is a fact about the date rather than a preference (12.243). What is left is one switch that picks the question and one that picks how much calendar a period covers. Counted in controls the panel has gone from four peers plus two modifiers to two switches; counted in what a reader has to know before they can read it, from three to none.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| 12.241                          | The **run-up reading is withdrawn**, not hidden: `leadTime.ts` and its axis are deleted rather than left behind a flag.                                                                                                                                                                                        | For one departure date, lead is the observation date subtracted from the flight date — so the points, the buckets and the band were the same numbers reversed and shifted, and 12.202 had already stopped calling them two charts. What it kept was a control that redrew one series against a different ruler, which is a thing a reader can want and not a thing this page is for: the question lead time was being asked was "is it cheaper to book earlier", and the honest answer needs many departures rather than one relabelled series. Deleted rather than dark-launched because a module nothing imports is a module nobody maintains, and the axis seam it was built on (`BucketAxis`, 12.170) survives untouched for the one axis that remains. 12.204's refusal to offer it to an unfocused watch goes with it, having nothing left to refuse.                                                                               |
| 12.242                          | Chart A is drawn **by day and only by day**; the period switch no longer touches it.                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Day, Week and Month over observation time bought a chart of ten or eleven points on an archive of 68 observations, and a week bucket is a band whose middle is a median of medians. What the chart exists to show is that a fare moved, and a day is the coarsest period that can still show it. It is also the only period on which the two series mean the same thing: the provider's baseline is one rounded figure a day, so a weekly bucket of it is an average of a summary. The switch stays on screen because it still drives chart B and the flight table underneath the panel — hiding it while chart A is open would move the head on every chart change, which is exactly what 12.246 forbids elsewhere.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| 12.243                          | Chart B's **source is chosen by the departure date**: the boards inside the watched month, the booking horizon outside it, and a period that straddles the boundary draws both **per day, in one frame**.                                                                                                      | The zoom made the reader answer "which archive holds this date" before they could look at it, and they have no way to know: what they know is the date. So the frame asks it per date. Inside the month the archive holds every itinerary at the hour it departs; outside it there is one cheapest fare per date and nothing else. A week crossing the end of the month is answered by both, which is the case the whole arrangement exists for and the one the zoom could not draw at all. The consequence is that the whole-horizon chart is gone — `CalendarCurveChart` and the folding in `lib/calendarCurve.ts` with it — because a curve date is now drawn as itself beside the boards and never folded into a week's median. Two answers to "what does this week cost" with nothing to choose between them would be worse than one. | 12.244                          | **A curve date is a span across its own date, and the axis changes resolution where the source does.** Day never leaves the watched month; Month therefore never mixes; Week is the only view that does, and the seam is drawn.                                                                                | A curve date has no time of day, so a point placed at midnight or at noon would assert a departure time the data does not have — the class of defect 12.230–12.237 spent a release removing. Drawn as a rule across the whole date, the mark says exactly what it knows. The axis follows: hours up to the last watched date, dates beyond it, with a dashed seam at the midnight the change happens on and a label under each stretch naming which archive answered. Day granularity is bounded to the departure dates the boards hold, which removes the degenerate case outright — there is no way to reach a single timeless number on a 24-hour clock. And a calendar month is either the watched one or it is not, so month granularity cannot mix; that is worth stating because a reader will otherwise look for the mixed case there and not find it. `scatterWindow`'s clip to the watched month goes, since the dates it used to crop are dates the horizon now prices.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| 12.245                          | **The price plate reads the series, not the pointer.** The vertical hairline still follows the cursor and names the date; the price snaps to what that date cost, and a date with no figure of ours gets no plate at all.                                                                                      | The plate used to print the pointer's own height as money, so a reader sweeping across the chart at a constant height read the same number on every day of the series — a fare nobody was quoted, in the app's money format, on the one element that looks like a quotation. An audit had already flagged it. Snapping to the series turns the crosshair from a ruler into a readout: moving along the chart now reads out each day's actual price, which is what a reader thought it was doing. Where the series has no figure, nothing is drawn — the answer 12.234 already gave the keyboard, and there is now no reason for the pointer to be the exception, because the pointer's height has stopped being a source of anything. The provider's baseline for that date is still named in the readout and in the live region, where it can be labelled as the provider's rather than mistaken for ours.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.246                          | **Chart B's name follows what it is drawing**, and the switch holds every name it can wear at once so the layout cannot move.                                                                                                                                                                                  | Twenty points in a day becoming one point a day is a change of kind, and a reader who is not told reads it as the flights having vanished — so the control says "Flights seen", "Cheapest per date" or "Flights and cheapest per date" according to the frame. A name that changes is a width that changes, and a switch that resized as the reader stepped through periods would push the chart and the table below it around at exactly the step they were comparing across. All four names are rendered in the same CSS grid cell with the three inactive ones held by `visibility: hidden`, so the button is as wide as its widest name whichever is showing. A `min-width` in pixels was the alternative and is a guess the next type-scale change breaks; the stack is the measurement itself. The frame's own sentence, its head and its horizon note are given fixed heights for the same reason.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| 12.247                          | **Adding a route collects its booking horizon**, in the background, and the route is added whatever the upstream does.                                                                                                                                                                                         | It is two requests and about four seconds per city pair, and without it chart B has nothing at all to draw outside the watched month — which since 12.243 is most of what that chart is for. A route added and then found blank everywhere except its own month reads as broken. This is the only collection on the page that happens by itself, and the exception is bought by the ratio: once per pair rather than once per departure, against a boards pass of up to thirty-one. The failure behaviour is the decision: the add is a write to the reader's own stored document and the collection is a request to somebody else's server, so a route that failed to save because a fare lookup failed would let an upstream veto a watchlist edit — and leave no row to retry from. The collection is therefore fired after the add lands, reported in words beside the watchlist, and never rolled back into; until it succeeds the chart says the horizon is not collected yet. `POST /api/fares/calendar/collect` answers 202 with a running document, exactly as the boards endpoint does since 12.210, so a form never waits on an upstream.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| 12.248                          | The horizon pass gets **its own single slot** beside the boards' one, and the two may overlap.                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | `CollectionRunner` keeps one slot because the request gap paces one loop and two loops halve it without anybody deciding to — and that argument applies to a second horizon pass, which is why there is a slot at all. It does not obviously survive being applied _across_ the two: a horizon pass is two requests and a boards pass is up to sixty-two, so they can be in flight together and briefly double the paced rate. Sharing one slot was the alternative and was rejected because it makes a newly added route wait out a four-minute boards pass for the curve the add exists to fetch. Two requests against a pass that is already minutes long is a rounding error on the rate and a large difference to the reader. Recorded rather than hidden: if the doubled pace ever shows up as a refusal, the shape to build is one queue both passes take a turn in, not a second gap. | 12.249                          | **A city pair with no curve on disk is always due**, whatever the cadence says; a pair that has one still waits for it.                                                                                                                                                                                        | The horizon cadence measures staleness from the last _look_, and a look that failed counts as one — which is right for the scheduled pass, where nobody is waiting and a route the provider is refusing today should be left alone until tomorrow. It is wrong for the press that just added the route. Measured rather than reasoned about: the first time this endpoint was pointed at the live provider it was refused with "the date range is too wide", the failure was recorded as a look, and the retry a minute later came back `not-due` — so a route added at that moment would have had nothing to draw outside its month for a day, which is exactly the state 12.247 exists to get out of. Nothing on disk means nothing for the cadence to protect, so the endpoint passes `every_minutes=0` for that case and leaves it alone otherwise. Deciding it at the endpoint rather than inside `due` keeps the scheduled pass's behaviour untouched, and each attempt still costs a human pressing "Add route", so it cannot loop. | 12.250                          | **Open, found while verifying 12.247 and deliberately not decided here: the booking horizon's far end has moved and every calendar collection is now failing.**                                                                                                                                                | `MAX_DEPARTURE_HORIZON_DAYS` is 330 and was measured on 2026-08-19, when +330 days was 2027-07-15 and answered — that is the exact `toDate` of the ARI-SCL curve on disk. Pointed at the live provider on 2026-08-20, the second of the two windows now asks to 2027-07-16 and comes back `status 3 (the date range is too wide)`; the whole curve then fails, because two windows are one observation of one year and half of one must not be stored. Probed directly to separate the two candidate causes: `2027-02-17..2027-07-16` is refused and `2027-02-17..2027-07-10` answers with 144 dates, so it is the far end and not the width. One day's sample cannot tell a rolling 329 from a fixed calendar limit near 2027-07-15, and guessing between them by editing the constant is the kind of unmeasured change this feature keeps having to undo. **Recommendation** — do not move the constant; make `_collect_calendar` narrow its last window and retry once on `the date range is too wide`, which is self-correcting whichever of the two is true, and record what the retry found so the limit is measured rather than believed. This is pre-existing and affects the scheduled `fares-collect` pass identically; what 12.247 changes is only that a reader now meets it while adding a route, and the row tells them so. | 12.251                          | The source rail names **each archive once, on the widest stretch that archive holds**, at the longest wording that fits it — and two labels are never allowed to cross.                                                                                                                                        | Labelling every stretch drew two labels through each other, found in a browser on the owner's own ARI-SCL. That watch carries a focus date, so the page narrows the boards to one departure and the week of 1-7 March is three runs — curve, board, curve — not the two every test had. The third run's `one price a date` was centred 94 units from the second's `every flight, at the hour it departs`, which is 47 units less than half their combined width: measured in the live DOM at 653.3-740.5 against 504.5-700.8. It did not look broken, it looked like garbled text under the axis, which is worse — the rail is what tells a reader that twenty points in a day becoming one point a day is a change of kind rather than the flights vanishing, and it failed silently. Naming a source twice was never worth anything anyway; the seams already say where the boundary is. Each label now takes its source's widest run, drops from `every flight, at the hour it departs` to `flights, by hour` where the full form will not fit inside that run, and is clamped into the plot. Where even the short form does not fit — one date of a thirty-one-date month is 21 units of track against 87 of glyphs — it is drawn overhanging rather than dropped, because the stretch a reader cannot identify from its marks is exactly the one whose name must stay. The non-crossing rule is enforced rather than argued from the geometry, since the defect was precisely a case nobody had enumerated: shorten both, and if the short forms still cross keep the source covering more of the frame — on a tie the boards, because a bar reads as a whole-date price on its own while a column of dots needs the clock named. `RAIL_CHAR_WIDTH` is 5.45, measured with `getComputedTextLength` in the live chart (87.3 for 16 glyphs, 196.3 for 36) rather than estimated, and it has to be a constant because jsdom does not implement that method — a rule that measured at draw time could not be tested. The seam stops above the rail for the same reason it exists: taken down to it, the two seams around a single board date ruled through the words twice.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| 12.252                          | The flight table's filters are **one row**: a price _range_, and one word for "no constraint".                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Measured in Chrome at the real panel, whose content box is 1099px: the bar was an equal-width grid of `minmax(10rem, 1fr)`, so the widest single option — `Afternoon 12–17`, fifteen characters — set the floor for all seven controls, five fitted across and two wrapped, and the bar stood **137px tall**. It is now a flex row of content-sized controls at **66px**, half the height for the same information. Three things paid for it. Min price and Max price became one `Price` control, two fields either side of a dash, which is one question asked once instead of two filters that happen to be related; it is a `div role="group"` rather than a `label`, because a label may name one control and this names two, and each field keeps its own `aria-label` so "Min price" still answers. Five differently-worded ways of saying "no constraint" — `All airlines`, `Any time`, `Any stops`, `Any length`, `Any move` — became `Any`, since a select is as wide as its widest option and every one of those repeated the label directly above it. And the clear button says `Clear` while still answering to "Clear filters", which WCAG 2.5.3 allows because the visible word opens the accessible name. On the widest board on disk — March 2027 LIM-SCL, whose airline column holds `Aerolineas Argentinas` — the six controls come to **963px**, or **1041px** with the clear button, both inside 1099. The row wraps rather than overflows below that: its min-content is **164px** against the table's 857, so the filter bar can never be the thing that puts a sideways scrollbar on this page. | 12.253                          | The board summary is **gone**, and the caption **moved under the rows** and gave its page number away.                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Two lines of prose stood between the filters and the first flight. `The board runs $158,79 to $254,44 across 3 airlines` is deleted outright and nothing went with it: the two ends of the board are now the two placeholders inside the price range, which is a better home than a sentence because it is also the control that narrows them, and "across 3 airlines" is the length of the airline select. The caption keeps the facts nothing else carries — how many flights the period held, which stretch of watching that is, and how many rows the filters took, decisions 8.8 and 8.41 — but it sits below the table on `caption-side: bottom`, still a `<caption>` and still `aria-live`, so a screen reader still meets it on entering the table and still hears the count change when a filter does. Its third sentence is deleted: `Page 1 of 2` was printed twice, once here and once beside the pager's own buttons three lines below, and one page number is enough — the pager keeps it, because that is where a reader who wants a different page is looking. The table can still say which page of how many; it just says it once. | 12.254                          | What the Change column means is **how many times we have looked**, not which stamp is newest.                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | A flight with one observation is either one nobody has looked at twice or one looked at ten times that never budged — `trackFlights` records a price only when it differs from the one before, so both come out the same shape. The old rule told them apart by asking whether the flight's first observation carried the newest `capturedAt` on the whole route, and 12.110 made that meaningless: a watched month is polled one departure at a time, 31 requests over about three minutes, so exactly one departure date owns the newest stamp and every flight on the other thirty is judged against a clock reading from a different request. Measured on the archive of 2026-08-19: **ARI-SCL holds 103 itineraries, every one seen exactly once, and 101 were filed as "Unchanged"** — a price never compared to anything, reported as a price that did not move. On the LIM-SCL March 2027 board the same fault moved **230 of 294 rows** into the wrong category. The count of sightings decides it now: seen once and still on the board is `First seen` and prints an em dash, because there is no basis for a comparison; seen twice or more at one price is `Unchanged` and prints **`0.0%`**, because that is a measurement and it must not look like the row above that has never been measured; seen at two prices is `Rose` or `Fell` with the percentage. Counting sightings rather than fixing the stamp to be per-departure — the two are equivalent, since a flight still on the board that has been seen once is by definition in the newest snapshot of its own departure, but one of them states the fact and the other tests a string for equality and hopes the collector stamped it the way we expect. `New` was renamed `First seen` for the same reason the rule changed: on a route collected for the first time this morning, "New" reads as a claim about the board when all it can honestly be is a claim about the archive. | 12.255                          | The `Departs` filter is labelled by its **hours alone** — `12–17`, not `Afternoon 12–17`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Fifteen monospace characters at 9.96px made this the widest control on the bar and the single reason the row could not hold every filter at once; five characters fit. They are also the half that cannot be misread: a reader who picks "Morning" has to guess whether 11:30 is in it and one who picks `06–11` does not, and the field above already says `Departs`, so the word was carrying no meaning the control had not already stated. The bands themselves are unchanged, and only the ones some flight actually leaves in are ever offered. | 12.256                          | A flight that has left the board says **`Gone`** in the Change column, and the column is **finally right-aligned**.                                                                                                                                                                                            | The audit was right that "off the board" was printing in a numeric column, and half-right about the fix. The word belongs there — "it is not offered any more" is an answer to "what has this fare done", where an em dash is the answer for a fare nobody has compared yet, and the two must not collapse into one glyph. What was wrong was the shape: `Off the board` is thirteen characters in a column whose widest number is seven, which would have added **52px to the table's 857px min-content** and moved the viewport at which this page starts scrolling sideways from about 1279px to about 1331px. `Gone` costs nothing — it is narrower than the `Change` heading — and it is set with `font-variant-numeric: normal` and muted, so it is a word standing in a column of figures rather than a figure that cannot be compared with them. Found while measuring it: `.table th, .table td { text-align: left }` is specificity 0-1-1 and the bare `.numeric` beside it is 0-1-0, so **every price and every percentage in this table has been left-aligned since the column was written** — the headings only looked right because `.numeric .sort` pushes the button's contents over, which made it read as a heading bug. Chrome reported `text-align: left` on the price cell of every row; `.table td.numeric` fixes it. | 12.257                          | The panel names itself **on the filter row** — `Flights seen · 09/03/2027` — and the row is one line **from a 1245px panel up**, which this panel is not.                                                                                                                                                      | The heading moved out of `AirfarePage` and into `FlightTable`, because a heading rendered in one file cannot share a line with controls rendered in another; all that crosses the boundary is the departure the reader picked, already formatted. It is a sibling of the filter group and not a member of it — a group a screen reader announces as "Filter flights" must not contain the panel's `<h2>` — and it is rendered outside the empty-state early return, since a panel whose heading disappears with its data reads as a panel that lost its name. The separator is the middot the watchlist row already uses between a pair and its month. **It does not fit, and the measurement is the answer rather than a shrunk control.** At the panel's real 1099px content box the heading is 273px and the six filters 962, so the line is **146px short**, or 224 with the clear button; on the narrowest board in the archive — ARI-SCL, one carrier, focused on a day, filtered — it is 66px short. One line needs **1245px** of panel, which is a viewport of about 1667 against the 1521 this machine has. Forcing it with `nowrap` was measured and rejected: `.filters` would shrink until a control fell to a second line, and a wrapped filter row costs 66px where the heading's own line costs 33, so the panel would end up **137px tall instead of 104** — taller, to obey an instruction whose point was to make it shorter. So the bar wraps until there is genuinely room, and becomes the single row the moment there is. What the move buys unconditionally is 20px: the heading's 15px bottom margin and the grid gap under it collapse into one 10px flex gap, and the table starts 140px below the panel's top where it used to start 160. What would have to give to close the last 146px, none of it this agent's to decide: the airline select is 251px on the March 2027 board because `Aerolineas Argentinas` is 21 characters and a select is as wide as its widest option (capping or abbreviating it buys ~129); `Up to 12h 30m` to `≤12h 30m` buys 50; four characters of price field instead of five buys 20; the heading at the controls' 0.85rem instead of 0.95 buys 28.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 12.258                          | The airline filter is **capped at eight characters**, with the cut marked and the whole name still reachable.                                                                                                                                                                                                  | A `<select>` is as wide as its widest option, so one long carrier sets the width of the control whether or not anyone ever picks it. `Aerolineas Argentinas` is 21 characters and carries **19 of the 1275 offers in the archive**, and it was spending **251px** of a row with 1099 — the bar was being laid out around the carrier least likely to be looked for. Eight characters is `JetSMART` exactly: the longest name in this archive the row can afford whole, leaving LATAM, Avianca and JetSMART — 1256 of those offers — untouched and cutting precisely one name, to `Aerolin…`. Measured after: the control is **122px**, saving the 129 predicted. A `max-width` on the select was tried first and rejected on measurement — Chrome ignores `text-overflow` on the closed control and simply cuts the glyphs, so the name read `Aerolineas Argenti` with nothing to say it had been shortened, which is a name a reader has no reason to doubt. The `…` is that reason, and the full name travels three ways: the option's `title`, the closed control's `title` once that carrier is chosen, and the Airline column of every row the filter keeps, which spells it out in full. A prefix can still collide where two carriers share an opening — `American Airlines` and `American Eagle` would both read `America…` — which is why the table below stays the place a name is read. **Where this leaves the one-line row of 12.257**, measured at the real 1099px: ARI-SCL unfiltered 1085, **one line at 66px**; ARI-SCL filtered 1164, 65 short; LIM-SCL March unfiltered 1115, **16 short**; LIM-SCL March filtered 1193, 94 short. The last 16px were looked for and are not there: every control on the row carries the same `5px 10px` padding and both gaps are 10px, so there is no outlier to reclaim, and the middot's 28.2px is already 5px _tighter_ than the `space · space` the rest of this page sets the same separator in. Taking the remainder from a control would be a squeeze the owner declined, and `nowrap` was already rejected in 12.257 for making the panel taller. So three of the four states stay honestly on two lines.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| 12.259                          | Whether there is **more above or below is read**, never assumed, and marked in front of the rows.                                                                                                                                                                                                              | A list that stops at a clean edge reads as the end of the list. The usual CSS-only answer — a shadow on the scroll container's background, covered at each end by a `background-attachment: local` gradient — is invisible here, because the rows are opaque cards and the background paints behind them; the mark has to be in front. Nothing in CSS can put an overlay in front of content _and_ know the scroll position. `animation-timeline: scroll()` can and is refused: where it is unsupported the animation never runs, so the mark either never appears or never goes away, and both are the page lying about what is there. So the position is read, and the reading is `lib/listEdge.ts` — three numbers in, one of four words out, unit-tested rather than eyeballed, with a pixel of slack because a fractional box scrolled to its end misses by a rounding error and a shadow that never quite switches off is the same lie as one always on. The component only calls it and writes the answer into `data-edge`; the stylesheet draws a darkening rather than a fade, so it needs no guess at the panel's gradient under the list. The scrollbar stays drawn `thin` beside it: the platform's own answer cannot be out of step with its box, and the shadow is the half that survives an overlay scrollbar fading out. The reachability is the other half — the rows hold buttons, so Tab walks in, but that is the only way in; the scroller carries its own tab stop and an accessible name so a reader who is reading rather than operating gets the arrow keys. | 12.260                          | The **focus date is gone**: a watched route is a city pair and a month, and the page reads the month.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Asked for by the owner, and it reverses 12.130 rather than trimming it — the whole apparatus goes, not the form control that produced it. A month was already the unit 12.110 chose for collection, and the argument for reading a narrower one was that a reader has one flight in mind; what a day of the watch actually bought them was a panel, a chart, a flight table and a heading that all showed one departure and hid the other thirty, from a precision they do not have at the moment they add the watch. So `focusDate` leaves `FareRoute`, `readingPrefix` and `formatReading` collapse into `route.month` and `formatFlightMonth(route.month)` at every caller, `focusDeparted` goes (a month that has gone is `route.month < monthOf(today)`, which the watchlist row and `collectableRoutes` already compare), and `setFocus` goes with the fourth transition on `useFareRoutes` that reached it. What survives untouched is the property all of it leaned on: `YYYY-MM` is a prefix of every departure key inside it, so `snapshotsFor` and the history endpoint still narrow with `startsWith` — 12.112. | 12.261                          | A stored focus **becomes simply its month**, and the migration is the normalizer not reading the key.                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Confirmed with the owner explicitly, because the other reading is tempting and wrong: pinning `2027-03-09` to `2027-03-01` keeps a day and throws away thirty, which is the opposite of what taking the focus away means, and the first of the month is a day nobody chose. Every entry written under 12.130 carries `month` beside `focusDate`, so the month is already there and already right — there is nothing to derive and nothing to invent. That is also why the migration belongs in `normalizeFareRoutes` and nowhere else: 12.133's rule still holds, and not reading a key needs no clock, invents nothing and writes nothing back. The stored JSON keeps its dead `focusDate` until the reader's next add, remove or reorder rewrites the document, and every read in between produces the same month. A migration that wrote on load would be a document editing itself on a page view. The owner's own watchlist is the case and is the test: `LIM→SCL 2027-03` with `focusDate: 2027-03-09` loads as `LIM→SCL 2027-03`, asserted through the hook as well as the normalizer so a `focusDate` surviving the read-then-write cycle fails too. | 12.262                          | The departure is **a month and a year, in two dropdowns**, defaulting to next month.                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Superseding 12.180's one exact date, which existed to produce the focus. The reader knows they are flying in September; the date control asked them for the 9th. Both dropdowns default to next month — the month they are standing in has lost its near days and its far ones barely move — and the default comes from one `nextMonth(today)` split into halves rather than from two reads of the clock, so December's roll moves the year with the month instead of leaving it eleven months behind. String arithmetic, never a `Date`: `new Date('2026-12-01')` is midnight UTC and reads as November in Lima. **They cannot disagree, and not by a guard.** 12.185's hazard was _redundancy_ — a day already states its month, so two editable values named one fact and one could be stale, invisibly, because `type="date"` renders `09/03/2027` identically whichever month sits above it. A month and a year hold no redundancy at all: neither states the other, together they name exactly one `YYYY-MM`, and both are spelled out on screen the whole time. | 12.263                          | The year list comes from the **collectable horizon**, not from two literals.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | `collectableYears(today)` spans today's year to the year `lastCollectableDay` lands in, which today is 2026 and 2027 exactly — `26` and `27` on screen, with the full `YYYY` as the option's value because a two-digit year is a century nobody stated. Written down, those two would be right until 2027 and then silently wrong, in the one control whose job is to stop a reader naming a month nobody can collect. It is **two years for most of the year and one for part of it**, which is correct rather than a shortfall: 330 days from a January date lands inside the same year, so in January there is no second year to offer. It starts at today's year rather than at the default month's, because the current month's remaining days are collectable and a reader must be able to say so. | 12.264                          | All twelve months stay on offer, and **a combination outside the horizon is refused by name**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | 12.184 is kept and restated for two controls. The alternative was narrowing one dropdown against the other, and it fails on the crossing case: with August picked, moving the year from 26 to 27 would have to un-pick the month — a control editing itself while the reader is looking at it — and a greyed-out option gives no reason where the message names the last day that works. So all twelve months and both years are always selectable, and `submit` refuses in the same voice and the same `role="alert"` as every other refusal this form makes: "Fares are only on sale as far ahead as 15/07/2027." for a month past the horizon, "That month has gone." for one behind this one — reachable because the year list starts at this year. A month the horizon lands _inside_ is taken: July 2027 holds fourteen collectable departures and seventeen that are not, and the collector already polls what it can reach and reports the rest by name, exactly as it does for the days of this month that have gone.
